at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
, then this contradicts the hypothesis,

\neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {z}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

If
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}
, then
from hypothesis
\neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x}
and
Lemma
54
, we
obtain
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{u^{\prime}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}
for some pop
o_{u^{\prime}}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}=at_{s}\ {o_{u^{\prime}}}
.

But from
\textsc{Miss}\ {z}\ {u^{\prime}}
we have

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {z}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{u^{\prime}}}})}
, which means
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {z}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}
.

Since
y\notin E
, Lemma
52
implies that there is a push
u_{y}\notin E

such that
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {y}
.

We want to show
\textsc{Miss}\ {z}\ {u_{y}}
. We already know
at_{s}\ {z}\ll at_{s}\ {y}=at_{s}\ {u_{y}}
, which means it remains
to show
\forall o_{2}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {z}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}
.
So, let
o_{2}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
. Then,
at_{s}\ {y}=at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
.
Therefore,
y=o_{2}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
.
But we already know
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {z}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}
. So,

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {z}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}
.

Since
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {y}
, we have,

\exists u_{y}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {z}\ {u_{y}}\wedge at_{s}\ {u_{y}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}

which contradicts hypothesis
\neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {z}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

Lemma 56
.

If
{x}\prec^{+}{\_}
and
x\notin E
, then
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

Proof B.26
.

Denote by
P(x,y)
the statement,

x\notin E\implies\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})

To prove
{x}\prec^{+}{y}\implies P(x,y)
(which proves the lemma), it
suffices to prove the two properties,

•

(Base case).
x\prec y\implies P(x,y)
.

•

(Inductive case).
P(x,y)\wedge P(y,z)\implies P(x,z)
.

We prove first the inductive case. By definition of
P(x,y)
and
P(y,z)
, we have,

\displaystyle x\notin E\implies\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})

\displaystyle y\notin E\implies\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})

but then we trivially have
P(x,z)
.

The base case follows from the following claim when
i=|\textsc{Elim}|
. We prove the claim by induction on
i
.

{claim*}

Let
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
. Let
x,y\in\textsc{Ev}
.
If
x\prec_{i}y
and
x\notin E
, then
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

For the base case (
i=0
), we check each possibility in
x\prec_{0}y
,

•

Case
{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{B}{y}
. From definition of
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{B}
,

at_{s}\ {x}=at_{s}\ {y}
for
x
a push and
y
a pop.
So,
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {x}})
.

Therefore, by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-imply-span}}
,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

•

Case
{x}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{B}{y}
. Notice that for each case in Definition
28
,
we have
\textit{defined}\,({at_{s}\ {x}})
. Therefore,
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-imply-span}}
,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

For the inductive case, let
x\prec_{i+1}y
and
x\notin E
.
We check each case in the definition of
x\prec_{i+1}y
,

•

Case
{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}^{E}{y}
. By IH,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
holds.

•

Case
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
. Hence,
x\in P_{i}
.

By Part 1 of Lemma
46
,

x\in E_{i}
. But by Part 2 of Lemma
46
,

x\in E_{0}=E
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}\wedge y\in P_{i}\wedge\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
.

By definition of
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, there is a
z
such that
x\mathrel{{\prec_{i}}^{*}}z
and
({z}\sqsubset{C\ {y}}\vee{z}\sqsubset{y})
.

If
x=z
, then either
{x}\sqsubset{C\ {y}}
or
{x}\sqsubset{y}
. In both cases
x\in T
. Therefore,
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::terminated-implies-span}}
,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

If
x\neq z
, then
x\mathrel{{\prec_{i}}^{+}}z
. In particular,
x\prec_{i}z^{\prime}\mathrel{{\prec_{i}}^{*}}z
for some
z^{\prime}
.
By the IH on
x\prec_{i}z^{\prime}
, we have
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

•

Case
x\in P_{i}\wedge y\notin E_{i}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
.

By Part 1 of Lemma
46
,

x\in E_{i}
. But by Part 2 of Lemma
46
,

x\in E_{0}=E
(Contradiction).

•

Case
{x}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i}^{E}{y}
. By IH,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
holds.

Lemma 57
.

If
x\in\overline{T}\setminus E
, then
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

Proof B.27
.

Since
x\in\overline{T}
, there is
y\in T
such that
x\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}y
.

If
x=y
, then
x\in T
, and by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::terminated-implies-span}}
,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

If
x\neq y
, then
x\mathrel{{\prec}^{+}}y
. From hypothesis
x\notin E
and
Lemma
56
,
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.

Lemma 58
.

Let
x,y\in\overline{T}\setminus E
. If
{x}\sqsubset{y}
, then
{\mathcal{S}\ {x}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {y}}
.

Proof B.28
.

From Lemma
57
, we have
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})

and
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})
. Hence, by
Lemma
51
,
{\mathcal{S}\ {x}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {y}}
.

Lemma 59
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-concurrent-lifo}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Proof B.29
.

From the fact that
u_{1},u_{2},o_{1}\in\overline{T}\setminus E
,
we know that
\mathcal{S}
is defined for the three events
by Lemma
57
.
In addition,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::span-implies-all-properties}}
implies
that function
at_{s}
is defined for these three events.

From the hypothesis

o_{1}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u_{2}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u_{1}
and Part 3 of Lemma
46
,
we have
o_{1}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{0}u_{2}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{0}u_{1}
.
In particular
o_{1}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}^{B}u_{2}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}^{B}u_{1}
.

Since
{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{1}}
and
u_{1},o_{1}\notin E_{0}
,
Lemma
47
implies
{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{0}{o_{1}}
, which in turn implies

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}}
by definition.
Hence, by Pop-Totality,

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
or
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
or

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.

The case
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
contradicts
u_{2}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}^{B}u_{1}
.
The case
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
leads to
u_{1}=u_{2}
by

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
, which contradicts
u_{2}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}^{B}u_{1}
.
Hence, we can assume
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.
So,
at_{s}\ {o_{1}}=at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.

Together with
o_{1}\not\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}^{B}u_{2}
, we have,

\displaystyle at_{s}\ {o_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {o_{1}}\ {u^{\prime}}\wedge at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}

(11)

From the second conjunct in (
11
) and
Lemma
54
(instantiating
u\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}u_{2}
), there is a pop
o_{2}\notin E

such that
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
.
Therefore,
{u_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{0}{o_{2}}
, and hence
{u_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{2}}
by Part 3 of
Lemma
46
.

Substituting
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
in (
11
),
we obtain
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{0}{o_{1}}
by definition, and hence
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{o_{1}}
by
Lemma
46
.

Since
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{o_{1}}
holds, this also implies that

o_{2}\in\overline{T}
since
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
14
),
and hence
o_{2}\in\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Lemma 60
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-pop-uniqueness}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Proof B.30
.

From
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{1}}
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{2}}
,
Lemma
47
implies

{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{0}{o_{1}}
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{0}{o_{2}}
(since
u,o_{1},o_{2}\notin E_{0}
), which in turn imply

at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}}
and
at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
by definition.

Therefore
at_{s}\ {o_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
(hence,
\textit{id}\ {o_{1}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{2}}
), which implies
o_{1}=o_{2}

by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
.

Lemma 61
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Proof B.31
.

Since
\overline{T}\setminus E\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\setminus E
,
by Lemma
55
, we need to consider four cases,

•

Case
x
and
y
are pushes and
at_{s}\ {x}\ll at_{s}\ {y}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubseteq{x}
. If
{y}\sqsubset{x}
, then,

\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
by Lemma
58
.
So,
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}

(Disjoint Push Timestamp Generation) implies
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}
.
Hence,
at_{s}\ {x}\ll at_{s}\ {x}
(Contradiction).

If
y=x
, then
at_{s}\ {x}\ll at_{s}\ {x}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x
is a push,
y
is a pop, and,

at_{s}\ {x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}\vee(at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x})

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubseteq{x}
. The case
y=x
leads to a contradiction, since
x
and
y
are
of different type. Hence, we can assume
{y}\sqsubset{x}
. So,
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
by Lemma
58
.

–

Case
at_{s}\ {x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
. Since
y\notin E
,
Lemma
52
implies that there is a push

u_{y}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {y}
and
{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{y}}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {y}}
.

But
{\mathcal{S}\ {y}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {x}}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{y}}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {x}}
. So, by
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}
(Disjoint Push Timestamp Generation) we have
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}\ll at_{s}\ {x}
, and we get the contradiction,

at_{s}\ {y}=at_{s}\ {u_{y}}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}

–

Case
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x}
.

From the case hypothesis and
Lemma
54
(instantiating with
u\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}x
), we get
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{x}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}

where
o_{x}\notin E
is a pop such that
at_{s}\ {o_{x}}=at_{s}\ {x}
.
By Lemma
52
, we also
have
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{x}}})}
. But then we have the contradiction
(using
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::start-end-of-span}}
),

end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{x}}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{x}}})<end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})

•

Case
x
is a pop,
y
is a push, and
\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubseteq{x}
. The case
y=x
leads directly to a contradiction since
x
and
y
are
of different type. Hence, we can assume
{y}\sqsubset{x}
. So,
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
by Lemma
58
.

By
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::vis-proof::misses-are-late}}
(Misses start late) with
\textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}
, we have

start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}})
.

Since
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
, we have
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}})
, which means
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}
.

So, by
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}
(Disjoint Push Timestamp Generation) we have
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}
, and we get the contradiction from hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

•

Case
x
and
y
are pops, and,

\displaystyle(\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y})\ \vee

\displaystyle(at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x})

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubseteq{x}
. We have four cases two consider,

–

Case
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

So,
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
by Lemma
58
.

By
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::vis-proof::misses-are-late}}
(Misses start late) with
\textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}
, we have

start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}})
.

Since
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
, we have
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}})
. Hence,
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}
.

Since
y\notin E
, Lemma
52

implies that there is a push
u_{y}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{y}}=at_{s}\ {y}
and

\mathcal{S}\ {u_{y}}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {y}
.
Hence (using
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::start-end-of-span}}
),

end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{y}}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}})

which means
\mathcal{S}\ {u_{y}}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {u^{\prime}}
.

So, by
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}
(Disjoint Push Timestamp Generation) we have
at_{s}\ {y}=at_{s}\ {u_{y}}\ll at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}
, and we get the contradiction from hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

–

Case
y=x
and
\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

From
\textsc{Miss}\ {x}\ {u^{\prime}}
we have
at_{s}\ {y}=at_{s}\ {x}\ll at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}
, and we get the contradiction
from hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {y}
.

–

Case
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x}
.

So,
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
by Lemma
58
.

Since
x\notin E
, Lemma
52

implies that there is a push
u_{x}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{x}}=at_{s}\ {x}
.

From the case hypothesis and Lemma
54
(instantiating with
u\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}u_{x}
), we get
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{x}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}
for some pop
o_{x}\notin E
such that

at_{s}\ {u_{x}}=at_{s}\ {o_{x}}
.
Hence,
at_{s}\ {x}=at_{s}\ {u_{x}}=at_{s}\ {o_{x}}
, which implies
x=o_{x}
by

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
, meaning

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}
.

But then we have the contradiction (using
\mathcal{S}\ {y}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {x}
and
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::start-end-of-span}}
),

end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})<end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})<start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})

–

Case
y=x
and
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}\ \wedge\ \neg\exists u^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textsc{Miss}\ {y}\ {u^{\prime}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ll$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ll$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}at_{s}\ {x}
.

We get the contradiction directly from case hypothesis
at_{s}\ {y}\ll at_{s}\ {x}=at_{s}\ {y}
.

Lemma 62
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-return-completion}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Proof B.32
.

Since
x\in\overline{T}\setminus E
,
Lemma
57

implies
\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {x}})
.
Also,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::span-implies-all-properties}}
implies

\textit{defined}\,({\textsc{O}({x})})
and
\textit{defined}\,({at_{s}\ {x}})
.

Define
v\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\textsc{O}({x})
. To show
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
, we need to consider the following cases,

•

Case
x
is a push.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}
implies
v=\textsc{O}({x})=tt
.

•

Case
x
is a pop and
v\neq\textsc{EMPTY}
.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}

implies that there is a push
u
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {x}

and
v=\textsc{O}({x})={u}.\textit{in}
.
Lemma
52
then
implies
at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {x}
.
Additionally, Lemma
48
implies

u\notin E
because of hypothesis
x\notin E
.

Hence,
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{0}{x}
by definition, which means
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{x}

by Lemma
46
.

Also, since
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{x}
, then
u\in\overline{T}
, since
\overline{T}
is

\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
).

•

Case
x
is a pop and
v=\textsc{EMPTY}
.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}

implies that there is a push
u
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {x}

and
v=\textsc{O}({x})={u}.\textit{in}\in\textsc{Val}
. Therefore,
v\in\textsc{Val}
,
which contradicts
v=\textsc{EMPTY}
, i.e., this simply means that
pops never return
EMPTY
, which means that this case is impossible.

Finally, if
x\in T
,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::terminated-implies-span}}

implies
v=\textsc{O}({x})={x}.\textit{out}
.

Theorem 63
.

All the visibility-style axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E
.

Proof B.33
.

Directly from the previous lemmas.

B.1.3
Putting Back Elimination Pairs

Given the relations in Definition
30
,
this section shows the inductive step of the proof:
if the visibility-style axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

then they also hold at domain

\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.
16
16

16

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}

has as hypothesis a transitive closure of the constraint relation
x\mathrel{{\prec}^{+}}y
.
We prove that if the axiom holds when the transitive closure is computed over the domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

then the axiom also holds when the transitive closure is computed over the domain

\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Finally, Theorem
72

shows that all the visibility-style axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}
.

Lemma 64
.

If
o=C\ {u}
, then
(\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {u}\Longleftrightarrow\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {o})
.

Proof B.34
.

We will do the forward implication. The other direction is similar.
From definition of
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {u}
, there is a
z
such that
x\prec_{i}^{*}z
and
(z\sqsubset u\vee z\sqsubset C\ {u})
.

But
C\ {u}=o
(so,
C\ {o}=u
by definition). Hence,

(z\sqsubset C\ {o}\vee z\sqsubset o)
holds as well, which is
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {o}
by definition.

Lemma 65
.

If
x,y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
,
y\prec_{i}^{*}x
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
, then
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

Proof B.35
.

By definition of
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
, there is
z^{\prime}
such that
x\prec_{i}^{*}z^{\prime}
and
(z^{\prime}\sqsubset z\vee z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z})
.
So, from
y\prec_{i}^{*}x
we obtain
y\prec_{i}^{*}z^{\prime}
, and therefore
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
by definition.

Lemma 66
.

Let
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.
Given the domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i+1}
for relation
\prec
,
if
{x}\prec^{+}{y}
, then one of the following cases hold,

•

x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
.

•

x\prec_{i}^{+}y
.

•

x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
.

•

x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

•

x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
.

Proof B.36
.

By repeating Lemma
47

on hypothesis
{x}\prec^{+}{y}
, we have

x\mathrel{{\prec_{i+1}}^{+}}y
in the domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Denote by
P(x,y)
the statement of the five cases in the lemma we want to prove.

To prove
x\mathrel{{\prec_{i+1}}^{+}}y\implies P(x,y)
, it
suffices to prove the two properties,

•

(Base case).
x\prec_{i+1}y\implies P(x,y)
.

•

(Inductive case).
P(x,y)\wedge P(y,z)\implies P(x,z)
.

For the base case, the cases in the definition of
x\prec_{i+1}y
are directly one of the conclusions
in the lemma. Hence, we focus on the inductive case. For this, we check each of the 25 cases in the hypothesis

P(x,y)\wedge P(y,z)
.

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, and
y
is a push and
z
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
.

Then,
y
is a pop and a push (Contradiction).

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, and
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

By definition,
x,y\in P_{i}
, so Lemma
46
implies
x,y\in E_{i}
.
From
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
y\notin E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, and
y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

By definition,
x,y\in P_{i}
, so Lemma
46
implies
x,y\in E_{i}

(Contradiction).

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, and
y,z\notin E_{i}
, and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

By definition,
x,y\in P_{i}
, so Lemma
46
implies
x,y\in E_{i}

(Contradiction).

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, and
y\in P_{i}
,
z\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

Since
C\ {y}=x
, by Lemma
64
with
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
, we get
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {x}
.
Also, since
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
, we have
x\in P_{i}
.

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
and
y
is a push and
z
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
.

By definition,
y\in P_{i}
, so Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
.
From
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
y\notin E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, and
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

Then trivially,
x\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, and
y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

By Lemma
65
, we obtain
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
.
Repeating Lemma
49

implies
x\notin E_{i}
. Also,
z\in P_{i}
by hypothesis.

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, and
y,z\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

By Lemma
65
, we obtain
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}
. But we also have
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

Repeating Lemma
49

implies
x\notin E_{i}
. Also,
z\notin E_{i}
by hypothesis.

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, and
y\in P_{i}
,
z\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
.
From
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
y\notin E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, and
y
is a push and
z
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
.

Since
C\ {y}=z
, by Lemma
64
with
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, we get
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
.
Also, since
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
, we have
z\in P_{i}
.

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, and
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
.
But from
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
y\notin E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, and
y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, and
y,z\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, and
y\in P_{i}
,
z\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

In other words,
x,z\notin E_{i}
, and
\exists y\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and
y
is a push and
z
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
.

By definition,
y\in P_{i}
, so Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

From
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
z\notin E_{i}
.
From
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, Lemma
65
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.
So,
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and
y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

If
z=z^{\prime}
, we would get
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
(Contradiction).

Hence,
z\neq z^{\prime}
. But
z,z^{\prime}\in P_{i}
, and
P_{i}
is a two-element set,
we must have
C\ {z}=z^{\prime}
by definition of
P_{i}
.
But then by Lemma
64
,

\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
(Contradicts
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
).

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and

y,z\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime\prime}}
.

If
z^{\prime}=z^{\prime\prime}
, then
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.
If
z^{\prime}\neq z^{\prime\prime}
, since
z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}\in P_{i}
, and
P_{i}
is a two-element set,
we must have
C\ {z^{\prime\prime}}=z^{\prime}
. But then by Lemma
64
,

\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime\prime}}
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and so
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, and

y\in P_{i}
,
z\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, and
y
is a push and
z
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(y,z)
.

By definition,
y\in P_{i}
. So, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, and
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
.

From
y\prec_{i}^{+}z
, repeating Lemma
49

implies
z\notin E_{i}
. From
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, Lemma
65
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {x}
.

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, and
y\notin E_{i}
,
z\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

If
x=z
, then
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
(Contradiction).

Hence,
x\neq z
. But since
x,z\in P_{i}
, and
P_{i}
is a two-element set,
we must have
C\ {z}=x
. But then by Lemma
64
,

\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
, which contradicts
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, and
y,z\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z^{\prime}\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

If
x=z^{\prime}
, then
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
(Contradiction).

Hence,
x\neq z^{\prime}
. But since
x,z^{\prime}\in P_{i}
, and
P_{i}
is a two-element set,
we must have
C\ {z^{\prime}}=x
. But then by Lemma
64
,

\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
, which contradicts
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z^{\prime}}
.

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, and
y\in P_{i}
,
z\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {z}\ {y}
.

From
y\in P_{i}
, Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
(Contradiction).

Lemma 67
.

We have the following facts,

1.

If
{x}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {y}
, then
{x}\not\sqsubset{y}
and
{y}\not\sqsubset{x}
.

2.

If
x=C\ {y}
, then
{x}\not\sqsubset{y}
and
{y}\not\sqsubset{x}

(equivalently
{C\ {y}}\not\sqsubset{y}
and
{y}\not\sqsubset{C\ {y}}
).

Proof B.37
.

We prove each fact in turn,

1.

{x}\not\sqsubset{y}
follows directly from the definition of
{x}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {y}
.

We now show
{y}\not\sqsubset{x}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubset{x}
.
The definition of
{x}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {y}
implies
\textit{id}\ {x}=\textit{id}\ {y}
. So,

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pop-not-precedes-push}}
implies

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}.\textit{time}\not<_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{start}
.
However, from
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::span-inside-event}}
(ii)
and the fact
{y}\sqsubset{x}
we obtain,

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {y}})}.\textit{time}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{y}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{start}
(Contradiction).

2.

If
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, then we have
{x}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {y}
(equivalently,
{(C\ {y})}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {y}
) and the result follows
by part 1. If
x
is a pop and
y
is a push, then we have
{y}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {x}
(equivalently,
{y}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {(C\ {y})}
) and the result follows
by part 1.

Lemma 68
.

Let
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.
If Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-concurrent-lifo}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, then
the axiom also holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Proof B.38
.

Let
u_{1},o_{1},u_{2}\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
. From hypothesis
{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{1}}
,
Lemma
47
implies

{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{o_{1}}
. Hence, by definition, we have two cases,

•

Case
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u_{1},o_{1})
. So,
u_{1},o_{1}\in P_{i}
by definition.

We must have
u_{2}\notin P_{i}
because
P_{i}
is a two element set
by definition, containing
already
u_{1}
and
o_{2}
, and we have
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
as hypothesis. Therefore, we must also have
u_{2}\notin E_{i}
,
otherwise we would have
u_{2}\in E_{i+1}
by definition (Contradicts hypothesis
u_{2}\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
).

From the hypothesis
o_{1}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u_{2}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u_{1}
,
Lemma
46
implies

o_{1}\not\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}u_{2}\not\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}u_{1}
(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
).
Expanding negations
in the definition of
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}
, we have the following two implications,

\displaystyle o_{1}\in P_{i}\wedge u_{2}\notin E_{i}\implies\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {u_{2}}\ {o_{1}}

\displaystyle u_{2}\notin E_{i}\wedge u_{1}\in P_{i}\implies\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {u_{2}}\ {u_{1}}

Hence,
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {u_{2}}\ {o_{1}}
and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {u_{2}}\ {u_{1}}
which is a
contradiction by Lemma
64
(since
C\ {u_{1}}=o_{1}
).

•

Case
{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{1}}
.
Lemma
49

implies
u_{1},o_{1}\notin E_{i}
.

We now consider two cases,

–

Case
u_{2}\in P_{i}
. By definition,
P_{i}
contains a push and a pop.
Let
o_{2}
be the second element in the set
P_{i}
, which is a pop such that

\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u_{2},o_{2})
, and so
{u_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{o_{2}}
by definition,
which means
{u_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{2}}
by Lemma
46
.

But from the hypothesis
o_{1}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u_{2}
,
Lemma
46
implies

o_{1}\not\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}u_{2}
(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
).
Expanding negations
in the definition of
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}
, we have the following implication,

o_{1}\notin E_{i}\wedge u_{2}\in P_{i}\implies\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {o_{1}}\ {u_{2}}

Therefore,
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {o_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
, but by Lemma
64
,

\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {o_{1}}\ {o_{2}}
also (because
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u_{2},o_{2})
and so
C\ {u_{2}}=o_{2}
).
Therefore,
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}{o_{1}}
by definition (because we also know

o_{1}\notin E_{i}
and
o_{2}\in P_{i}
).
So,
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{o_{1}}

by Lemma
46
since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.

We must also have
o_{2}\in\overline{T}
, since
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{o_{1}}
, and
o_{1}\in\overline{T}
, and

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
).
In addition,
o_{2}\notin E_{i+1}
, for otherwise
o_{2}\notin P_{i}
, which is a contradiction.

–

Case
u_{2}\notin P_{i}
. Therefore,
u_{2}\notin E_{i}
as well, for otherwise
we would have
u_{2}\in E_{i+1}
by definition (contradicting the hypothesis
u_{2}\notin E_{i+1}
).

Since
u_{1},u_{2},o_{1}\notin E_{i}
and the axiom holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
,
we obtain
{u_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{2}}
and
{o_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{o_{1}}
for some pop

o_{2}\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
. But Lemma
46

implies
o_{2}\notin E_{i+1}
also (since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}i+1
).

Lemma 69
.

Let
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.
If Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-pop-uniqueness}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, then
the axiom also holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Proof B.39
.

From the hypotheses
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{1}}
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o_{2}}
,
Lemma
47

implies
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{o_{1}}
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{o_{2}}
.
From the definition of
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}
, we consider four cases,

•

Case
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o_{1})
and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o_{2})
.
Hence
(u,o_{1})=(u,o_{2})
, which implies
o_{1}=o_{2}
.

•

Case
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o_{1})
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{2}}
.

By definition
u\in P_{i}
. Lemma
46

implies
u\in E_{i}
.
But from
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{2}}
,
Lemma
49

implies
u\notin E_{i}

(Contradiction).

•

Case
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{1}}
and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o_{2})
.
Similar to the previous case.

•

Case
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{1}}
and
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i}{o_{2}}
.

Lemma
49

implies
u,o_{1},o_{2}\notin E_{i}
.
Since the axiom holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, it follows
o_{1}=o_{2}
.

Lemma 70
.

Let
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.
If Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, then
the axiom also holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Proof B.40
.

Since
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i+1}
,
Lemma
66
implies that we need to consider five cases,

•

Case
x
is a push and
y
is a pop, and
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(x,y)
.

Directly from Lemma
67
,
we have
{y}\not\sqsubset{x}
. While
x\neq y
holds because
x
and
y
have different types.

•

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}y
.

By repeating Lemma
49
we have
x\prec_{i}^{+}y

in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. Since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, repeating
Lemma
46

implies
x\prec^{+}y
in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. But

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
),
and since
y\in\overline{T}
by assumption,

x\prec^{+}y
holds in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.
Finally, since
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
we have

{y}\not\sqsubseteq{x}
.

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
,
y\in P_{i}
, and
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
x=y
. From
y\in P_{i}
,
Lemma
46
implies
y\in E_{i}
, which
means
x\in E_{i}
(Contradiction). Hence,
x\neq y
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
{y}\sqsubset{x}
.

By definition of
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {y}
, there is
z
such that
x\prec_{i}^{*}z
and
(z\sqsubset y\vee z\sqsubset C\ {y})
. From
x\prec_{i}^{*}z
we have either
x=z
or
x\prec_{i}^{+}z
.
Hence, we have four cases,

–

Case
x=z
and
z\sqsubset y
. So,
x=z\sqsubset y\sqsubset x
(Contradiction).

–

Case
x=z
and
z\sqsubset C\ {y}
. So,
y\sqsubset x=z\sqsubset C\ {y}
.
But
y
and
C\ {y}
are elimination pair couples, so
y\not\sqsubset C\ {y}
from Lemma
67
(Contradiction).

–

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}z
and
z\sqsubset y
. By repeating
Lemma
49
we have
x\prec_{i}^{+}z

in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. Since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, repeating
Lemma
46

implies
x\prec^{+}z
in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. But

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
),
and since
z\in T\subseteq\overline{T}
,

x\prec^{+}z
holds in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.

But from
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
z\sqsubset y
and the interval property of

\sqsubset
(Lemma
9
), we get
y\sqsubset y
(Contradiction) or

z\sqsubset x
which contradicts

C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
on the domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

(since
x\prec^{+}z
).

–

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}z
and
z\sqsubset C\ {y}
.
By repeating
Lemma
49
we have
x\prec_{i}^{+}z

in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. Since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, repeating
Lemma
46

implies
x\prec^{+}z
in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. But

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
),
and since
z\in T\subseteq\overline{T}
,

x\prec^{+}z
holds in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.

But from
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
z\sqsubset C\ {y}
and the interval property of

\sqsubset
(Lemma
9
), we get

y\sqsubset C\ {y}
or
z\sqsubset x
.

The case
y\sqsubset C\ {y}
is a contradiction because
y
and
C\ {y}

are elimination pair couples, so
y\not\sqsubset C\ {y}
from Lemma
67
.

The case
z\sqsubset x
contradicts

C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
on the domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

(since
x\prec^{+}z
).

•

Case
x,y\notin E_{i}
and
\exists z\in P_{i}.\ \textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}\wedge\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
.

Lemma
46

implies
P_{i}\subseteq E_{i}\subseteq E_{0}
. So,
from
z\in P_{i}
, we have
z\in E_{0}=E
.

We must have
x\neq y
because otherwise
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
y\sqsubset x
.

After unfolding the definition of
BE
in
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {z}
, doing some de Morgan manipulations and the fact that
z\in E
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\forall w.\ y\mathrel{{\prec_{i}}^{*}}w\implies({w}\not\sqsubset{C\ {z}}\wedge{w}\not\sqsubset{z})

(12)

By definition of
\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {z}
, there is
z^{\prime}
such that
x\prec_{i}^{*}z^{\prime}
and
(z^{\prime}\sqsubset z\vee z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z})
. From
x\prec_{i}^{*}z^{\prime}
we have either
x=z^{\prime}
or
x\prec_{i}^{+}z^{\prime}
.
So, we have four cases,

–

Case
x=z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset z
. So,
y\sqsubset x=z^{\prime}\sqsubset z
.
But this contradicts (
12
) when instantiated with
w\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}y
.

–

Case
x=z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z}
. So,
y\sqsubset x=z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z}

But this contradicts (
12
) when instantiated with
w\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}y
.

–

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset z
. By repeating
Lemma
49
we have
x\prec_{i}^{+}z^{\prime}

in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. Since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, repeating
Lemma
46

implies
x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. But

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
),
and since
z^{\prime}\in T\subseteq\overline{T}
,

x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
holds in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.

But from
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset z
and the interval property of

\sqsubset
(Lemma
9
), we get

y\sqsubset z
or
z^{\prime}\sqsubset x
.

The case
y\sqsubset z
contradicts (
12
)
when instantiated with
w\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}y
.

The case
z^{\prime}\sqsubset x
contradicts

C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
on the domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

(since
x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
).

–

Case
x\prec_{i}^{+}z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z}
. By repeating
Lemma
49
we have
x\prec_{i}^{+}z^{\prime}

in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. Since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, repeating
Lemma
46

implies
x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
in domain
\textsc{Ev}\setminus E_{i}
. But

\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
),
and since
z^{\prime}\in T\subseteq\overline{T}
,

x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
holds in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.

But from
{y}\sqsubset{x}
and
z^{\prime}\sqsubset C\ {z}
and the interval property of

\sqsubset
(Lemma
9
), we get

y\sqsubset C\ {z}
or
z^{\prime}\sqsubset x
.

The case
y\sqsubset C\ {z}
contradicts (
12
)
when instantiated with
w\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}y
.

The case
z^{\prime}\sqsubset x
contradicts

C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
on the domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}

(since
x\prec^{+}z^{\prime}
).

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
,
y\notin E_{i}
, and
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
.

Lemma
46

implies
P_{i}\subseteq E_{i}\subseteq E_{0}
. So,
from
x\in P_{i}
, we have both
x\in E_{i}
and
x\in E_{0}=E
.

We must have
x\neq y
, otherwise,
x=y\notin E_{i}
(Contradicting
x\in E_{i}
).

After unfolding the definition of
BE
in
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {y}\ {x}
, doing some de Morgan manipulations and the fact that
x\in E
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\forall w.\ y\mathrel{{\prec_{i}}^{*}}w\implies({w}\not\sqsubset{C\ {x}}\wedge{w}\not\sqsubset{x})

So, instantiating with
w\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}y
, we obtain
{y}\not\sqsubset{x}
as required.

Lemma 71
.

Let
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
.
If Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-return-completion}}
holds
at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, then
the axiom also holds at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Proof B.41
.

Let
x\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
. Since
x\notin E_{i+1}
, by definition of

E_{i+1}
, either
x\in P_{i}
or
x\notin E_{i}
.

•

Case
x\in P_{i}
.

If
x
is a push, then by definition of
P_{i}
,

{x}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {(C\ {x})}
,
and so
\textit{id}\ {x}=\textit{id}\ {(C\ {x})}
. If
x
is a pop, then

{(C\ {x})}\ \textsc{Elim}\ {x}
, and so
\textit{id}\ {x}=\textit{id}\ {(C\ {x})}
.
In both cases,
\textit{id}\ {x}
is defined, and from

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}
,
\textsc{O}({x})

is also defined.

Define
v\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\textsc{O}({x})
. To show
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
, we need to consider the following cases,

–

Case
x
is a push.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}
implies
v=\textsc{O}({x})=tt
.

–

Case
x
is a pop and
v\neq\textsc{EMPTY}
.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}

implies that there is a push
u
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {x}

and
v=\textsc{O}({x})={u}.\textit{in}
.

But
x\in P_{i}
, and by definition of
P_{i}
,

\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u^{\prime},x)
for some push
u^{\prime}
. Hence,

\textit{id}\ {u^{\prime}}=\textit{id}\ {x}
by definition of
Elim
.
Therefore,
\textit{id}\ {u^{\prime}}=\textit{id}\ {x}=\textit{id}\ {u}
, which means

u=u^{\prime}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
.

Since
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u^{\prime},x)=(u,x)
, we have
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{x}
by definition.
So, Lemma
46
implies
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{x}

(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
).

Also, since
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{x}
, then
u\in\overline{T}
, because
\overline{T}
is

\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
).
Finally, since
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,x)
, we have by definition
u\in P_{i}
.
Therefore,
u\notin E_{i+1}
, for otherwise we would have
u\notin P_{i}

by definition of
E_{i+1}
. In other words,
u\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

–

Case
x
is a pop and
v=\textsc{EMPTY}
.

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}

implies that there is a push
u
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {x}

and
v=\textsc{O}({x})={u}.\textit{in}\in\textsc{Val}
. Therefore,
v\in\textsc{Val}
,
which contradicts
v=\textsc{EMPTY}
, i.e., this simply means that
pops never return
EMPTY
, which means that this case is impossible.

Finally, if
x\in T
,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::terminated-implies-span}}

implies
v=\textsc{O}({x})={x}.\textit{out}
.

•

Case
x\notin E_{i}
.
We know the axiom holds on domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.
In other words, there is a
v
such that
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
and
x\in T\implies v={x}.\textit{out}
.

Notice that the cases in
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
hold in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
, so
we still need to show that the cases in
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
hold in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.
We consider the cases,

–

Case
x
is a push.

From
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
(in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
), we have
v=tt
.

–

Case
x
is a pop and
v\neq\textsc{EMPTY}
.

From
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
(in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
),
there is
u\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
such that
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{x}

and
v={u}.\textit{in}
.
But Lemma
46

implies
E_{i+1}\subseteq E_{i}
(since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}i+1
),
which means
u\notin E_{i+1}
, i.e.,
u\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

–

Case
x
is a pop and
v=\textsc{EMPTY}
.

Let
u\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
such that
x\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u
.
By definition of
E_{i+1}
, either
u\in P_{i}
or
u\notin E_{i}
.

*

Case
u\in P_{i}
. By definition of
P_{i}
,

\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o)
for some pop
o
. In particular,
we have
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{i+1}{o}
by definition.
So, Lemma
46
implies
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o}

(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
).

It remains to show that
{o}\mathrel{\ltimes}{x}
. From hypothesis
x\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}u
,
Lemma
46
implies

x\not\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}u

(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
). After some De Morgan manipulations on

x\not\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}u
,

x\notin E_{i}\wedge u\in P_{i}\implies\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {u}

So,
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {u}
. But
o
is the elimination couple of
u
, so
Lemma
64
implies
\neg\textsc{BE}_{i}\ {x}\ {o}
.
And since
o\in P_{i}
and
x\notin E_{i}
, we have

{o}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{i+1}{x}
by definition.
Hence, Lemma
46
implies
{o}\mathrel{\ltimes}{x}

(since
i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
).

Also, since
{o}\mathrel{\ltimes}{x}
, then
o\in\overline{T}
, because
\overline{T}
is

\prec
-downward closed (Lemma
14
).
Finally, since
\textsc{Elim}_{i}=(u,o)
, we have by definition
o\in P_{i}
.
Therefore,
o\notin E_{i+1}
, for otherwise we would have
o\notin P_{i}

by definition of
E_{i+1}
. In other words,
o\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

*

Case
u\notin E_{i}
.

From
\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
(in domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
), there is a pop

o\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
such that
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{o}
and
{o}\mathrel{\ltimes}{x}
.
But then, Lemma
46

implies that
o\notin E_{i+1}
(since
i<_{\mathbb{N}}i+1
and

o\notin E_{i}
). In other words,
o\in\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Theorem 72
.

All the visibility-style axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}
.

Proof B.42
.

We prove by induction on
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|

that all the axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
.

The base case
i=0
is Theorem
63
.

The inductive case are all the previous lemmas that show that
if the axioms are true at domain

\overline{T}\setminus E_{i}
(for
i<_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
, since

i+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\textsc{Elim}|
by hypothesis),
then they also hold at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{i+1}
.

Therefore, the axioms hold at domain
\overline{T}\setminus E_{|\textsc{Elim}|}
.
But Lemma
50

implies
|E_{|\textsc{Elim}|}|=2(|\textsc{Elim}|-|\textsc{Elim}|)=0
, i.e.,

E_{|\textsc{Elim}|}=\emptyset
.
Therefore, all axioms hold at
\overline{T}\setminus E_{|\textsc{Elim}|}=\overline{T}
.

Theorem 73
.

Given the relations in Definition
30
.
If all the TS-stack invariants of Figure
10
hold,
then the visibility-style axioms in Figure
3
hold.

Proof B.43
.

The hypothesis states that the TS-stack invariants hold. Therefore, the result follows
by Theorem
72
.

B.2
Proof of TS-Stack invariants

In the following subsections
we will define the pop-total strict partial order
\ll

and show that the invariants in Figure
10
hold
for the atomic timestamp TS-stack (Figures
6
,

7
and
8(a)
)
and the interval timestamp TS-stack (Figures
6
,

7
and
8(b)
).

B.2.1
Version: Atomic Timestamps

For the atomic TS-stack, we define
\ll
simply as,
17
17

17

Recall that we overloaded the symbol
<_{\textsc{T}}

to apply to both timestamps and abstract timestamps in Definition
25
.

\ll\ \mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\ <_{\textsc{T}}

First we prove the structural invariants, since they do not depend on
\ll
.
In this way, we can freely use the structural invariants in the rest of the lemmas.

Lemma 74
.

All the structural invariants in Figure
10
hold for the atomic timestamp TS-stack.

Proof B.44
.

Most of them are trivial, and follow directly from Definition
25
.
We focus only on the ones that require a little bit of more explanation.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pop-not-precedes-push}}
, since a pop takes a node with some id,
such node must have been inserted before by a push (no other procedure can link new nodes into the pool).
Since a push’s span links the node
into the pool as is first rep event, the push’s span cannot start after the pop took the node
(i.e., the node must be inserted into the pool before a pop can take such node, otherwise the pop
would not have been able to discover the node in the first place).

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-equal}}
, since the push finished its span before the pop started
its last iteration, the push assigned the generated timestamp to the node as its last rep event, before the pop started its last
iteration.
Therefore, when during its iteration, the pop reads at line
24

the timestamp of the node, at that moment, the node already has the timestamp assigned by the push.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::terminated-implies-span}}
, we need to check for pushes and pops.
If a push
x
terminated, then the executing thread completed the lines defining the push span, and the procedure
finished with the trivial output
tt
(there is an implicit return), and so, the definition
of
\textsc{O}({x})
coincides with the output of the event
{x}.\textit{out}
.
If a pop
x
terminated, the executing thread must have executed a successful
CAS at line
71
, otherwise it would have looped infinitely,
meaning that the executing thread had a last iteration and therefore, completed the lines defining its span.
After the successful CAS, the procedure reads the value stored in the taken node and returns, and so

\textsc{O}({x})
coincides with the output of the event
{x}.\textit{out}
.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}
, if two events
x
and
y
generate the same span,
then they executed the same rep events inside the spans. This implies that both events must have been executed
by the same thread (a rep event, being atomic, can only be executed by a single thread).
Suppose for a contradiction that
x\neq y
. Since events executed by the same thread appear sequentially and
disjoint in the history, then either
x

executes completely before
y
or
y
executes completely before
x
. But this implies that the rep events
inside the events cannot be the same (Contradiction). Therefore,
x=y
.
A similar argument applies to
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::reps-are-injective}}
.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
since ids are atomically
generated and assigned to fresh nodes, they are unique per push and also per node.
Hence, if two pushes have the same id, it means that they executed the same rep event,
leading to conclude that the pushes must be the same by following an argument similar to

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}
above.
If two pops have the same id, then they took the same node, since ids are also unique per node.
This means that both pops executed the same CAS rep event that set the
taken
flag at
line
71

(once the
taken
flag is set, it cannot be reset, since there are no instructions
that could change the boolean back to
false
). Therefore, both pops executed the same rep event,
leading to conclude that the pops must be the same by following an argument similar to

I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}
above.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::span-inside-event}}
all the events execute rep events inside the duration
of the event because all the rep events inside the event are executed by the same thread that invoked the event,
as such, the unique span that events execute, does it so within the duration of the event.

For
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pops-take-pushes}}
(i), when a pop finishes its span (i.e., takes a node),
such node must have been inserted by a push and the output of the pop will be the value stored in the node
(line
79
).

Lemma 75
.

\ll
is a pop-total strict partial order on abstract timestamps.

Proof B.45
.

By Lemma
26
,
18
18

18

Note that
the proof of Lemma
26

does not make use of any invariants, because the result
follows directly from definitions. Therefore, we can use
Lemma
26
safely in our proofs.

<_{\textsc{T}}
is still a strict partial order when it is extended to abstract timestamps.
It remains to show that
\ll
is pop-total.

Let
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=(\textit{id}\ {u_{1}},t_{s}\ {u_{1}})
and
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=(\textit{id}\ {u_{2}},t_{s}\ {u_{2}})
be the generated abstract timestamps for pushes
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
The generated standard timestamps
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}
and
t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
are natural numbers, because
the
newTimestamp
method in Figure
8(a)

returns a natural number. Therefore, we must have either
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}

or
t_{s}\ {u_{2}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{1}}
or
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}=t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.

The case
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}

trivially leads to
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=(\textit{id}\ {u_{1}},t_{s}\ {u_{1}})<_{\textsc{T}}(\textit{id}\ {u_{2}},t_{s}\ {u_{2}})=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}

from the overloading of
<_{\textsc{T}}
to abstract timestamps.

The case
t_{s}\ {u_{2}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{1}}
leads to a similar result.

For the case
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}=t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
,
since timestamps are atomic natural numbers, timestamps are unique per push.
This means that pushes
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
must have executed the same rep event that
created the timestamp.
The rest of the argument is as in the proof of
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}

in Lemma
74
.
This means that
u_{1}=u_{2}
, and hence,
they must have the same id, i.e.,
\textit{id}\ {u_{1}}=\textit{id}\ {u_{2}}
. In other words, we have,

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=(\textit{id}\ {u_{1}},t_{s}\ {u_{1}})=(\textit{id}\ {u_{2}},t_{s}\ {u_{2}})=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}

Therefore,
\ll
is pop-total.

Lemma 76
.

In a pool, at any moment, all untaken nodes reachable from the top have strictly decreasing timestamps. In other words,
let
t_{n}
and
t_{m}
be the timestamps of untaken nodes
n
and
m
, respectively. If
m
occurs after
n
when
traversing the linked list starting from the top, then
t_{m}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{n}
.

Proof B.46
.

Insertions to a pool are single-threaded, i.e., all nodes in a pool were inserted by the same thread. This implies that two
nodes in the pool were assigned timestamps generated by disjoint calls to the
newTimestamp
procedure, and the nodes were inserted
in the same order as the calls to the
newTimestamp
procedure. In other words, the most recently inserted node will have a bigger
timestamp. It remains to see if the unlinking code preserves the decreasing timestamp property.

The loop at lines
65
-
66

exits only when it reaches the sentinel node (stored in the
next
variable) or
the first untaken node (also stored in the
next
variable). In either case, when the loop ends,
the procedure sets the new node’s next
n.next
to
the found node in
next
. In case the found node is the sentinel node, then the pool will only contain the new node, which trivially
satisfies the strictly decreasing timestamp property. In case the found node is an untaken node, then the pool still
satisfies the decreasing timestamp property, since the new node
n
has the freshest timestamp and
n
is the new top.

Lines
72
-
78

carry out unlinking of nodes around the node
n
that was taken. Variable
oldTop

stores the top that the pool had when
n
was found. If the current top has not changed from
oldTop

(meaning that no new nodes were inserted into the pool), the CAS at line
72

will set as the new top the taken node
n
. This action preserves the decreasing timestamp property, because the property was true
before changing the top to one of the nodes in the pool (even if the new top has been marked as taken already).
Lines
73
-
74

unlink nodes between the old top and the taken node
n
, which preserves the decreasing timestamp property,
as it is only removing nodes.
Similarly, lines
76
-
78

unlink nodes between the taken node
n
and the first untaken node after
n
(or the sentinel node
if every node after
n
is already taken). This also preserves the decreasing timestamp property,
as it is only removing nodes.

Lemma 77
.

Untaken nodes do not get unlinked. In other words, if
n
is an untaken node that was inserted into a pool, then it remains reachable from the top
of the pool.

Proof B.47
.

When inserting a new node in the
insert
procedure in Figure
7
,
the new node is set as the top at line
63
.
It remains to check that the unlinking code does not remove untaken nodes.

The loop at lines
65
-
66

exits only when it reaches the sentinel node (stored in the
next
variable) or
the first untaken node (also stored in the
next
variable). In either case, when the loop ends,
the procedure sets the new node’s next
n.next
to
the found node in
next
. Therefore, any node between the new node and the node in
next
is already taken
and can be unlinked.

Lines
72
-
78

carry out unlinking of nodes around the node
n
that was taken. Variable
oldTop

stores the top that the pool had when
n
was found. If the current top has not changed from
oldTop

(meaning that no new nodes were inserted into the pool), the CAS at line
72

will set as the new top the taken node
n
(i.e., since
n
was the first untaken node to be found and the top
has not changed, then any node between the top and node
n
is taken and can be unlinked).
If the CAS fails, it means that new nodes were inserted before
oldTop
and so, we cannot unlink nodes
before
oldTop
. But nodes between
oldTop
and
n
are already taken (since
n
was the first untaken
node when the pool was traversed starting from
oldTop
), and therefore, these nodes can be unlinked
(lines
73
-
74
).
Finally, the loop in lines
76
-
78

unlink nodes between the taken node
n
and the first untaken node after
n
(or the sentinel node
if every node after
n
is already taken). Hence, these nodes can be unlinked.

Lemma 78

(Misses Lemma)
.

If,

•

o,u\notin E
,

•

t_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u}
, and

•

\forall o^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}
,

then
{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u}})}
.

Proof B.48
.

Let
maxT_{1}
,
maxT_{2}
, …,
maxT_{n}

be the strictly increasing sequence of timestamps assigned to the
maxT
variable during the last iteration of pop
o
(excluding
the initialization assignment at line
17
).
Similarly, let
chosen_{1}
,
chosen_{2}
, …,
chosen_{n}
and
chPool_{1}
,
chPool_{2}
, …,
chPool_{n}
be the sequences of assignments
to variables
chosen
and
chPool
(excluding initializing assignments at
lines
16

and
19
). All three sequences have
the same length
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
because variables
maxT
,
chosen
, and
chPool
are assigned together at
lines
28
-
29
.

Claim: Pop
o
cannot reach line
26
because
o
is not in an elimination pair
by hypothesis (i.e.,
o\notin E
).
Indeed, if
o
executed such line, then we must have
t_{start}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{g}

(the condition at line
25
),
where
t_{g}
is the timestamp of the node
g
taken by
o
and
t_{start}
is the timestamp generated by
o
at
line
10
.
Node
g
was inserted by some push
u_{g}
, which
means we have
\textit{id}\ {u_{g}}=\textit{id}\ {o}
. But since
o\notin E
, we also have
{u_{g}}\sqsubset{o}
,
meaning that the calls to
newTimestamp
executed by
u_{g}

(line
6
) and by
o

(line
10
) are disjoint, and
so
t_{g}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{start}
(Contradiction).
This proves the claim.

Hence, pop
o
took node
chosen_{n}
from pool
chPool_{n}
and with timestamp
maxT_{n}
after scanning all the pools
(i.e., pop
o
cannot prematurely end the scan of the pools by entering line 26).
Additionally, hypothesis
o\notin E
implies that the last assignment
maxT_{n}
must be a finite timestamp, because
the push that inserted
chosen_{n}
executed before
o
, meaning that
u
assigned the finite timestamp
to the node before
o
started the scan. This implies that every timestamp in the sequence

maxT_{i}
is finite, because it is a strictly increasing sequence and
maxT_{n}
is the last assignment.

The third hypothesis to the lemma implies that the node
m
inserted by
u
remains untaken during the last loop of
o
,
while the second hypothesis states that
m
was assigned a bigger timestamp
t_{m}
than
maxT_{n}
. Denote by
p_{m}
the pool where node
m
was inserted.
Notice that, during the last loop of
o
, node
m
could
have infinite timestamp or finite timestamp
t_{m}
, depending on whether or not the span of
u
finished during the last loop of
o
.

Assume for a contradiction that the span of
u
started before the span of
o
. This means
that node
m
was in the pool before
o
started its last loop.

Claim: Node
m
must be different from each
chosen_{i}
. If
m
is equal to
chosen_{i}
for some
i
, then
we have
t_{m}=maxT_{i}\leq_{\textsc{T}}maxT_{n}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{m}
(contradiction). Notice that
m
must have finite
timestamp at the moment it is discovered (hence, equal to
t_{m}
), because
m
is
chosen_{i}
, and each one of the
chosen_{i}
nodes had finite timestamp
when they were discovered. This proves the claim.

Claim: Pool
p_{m}
is different from each
chPool_{i}
. Suppose for a contradiction that
p_{m}
is equal to
chPool_{i}
for some
i
.
Since
m
remains untaken during
o
, when
o
finds node
chosen_{i}
at line
48
,
nodes
m
and
chosen_{i}
were simultaneously untaken in the same pool at that moment.
Hence, Lemma
77
implies that nodes

m
and
chosen_{i}
were reachable from the top at that moment,
which means that Lemma
76
implies

t_{m}<_{\textsc{T}}maxT_{i}
, since
chosen_{i}
was the first to be found and
m
is different from
chosen_{i}

by the previous claim.
Hence,
t_{m}<_{\textsc{T}}maxT_{i}\leq_{\textsc{T}}maxT_{n}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{m}
(contradiction).
This proves the claim.

If pool
p_{m}
is in between
chPool_{i}
and
chPool_{i+1}
, then
o
must have
picked
m
(or some other node with even bigger timestamp), since
m
has either infinite timestamp or finite timestamp but bigger than

maxT_{i}
and
maxT_{i+1}
(since
maxT_{n}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{m}
). If
p_{m}
is a pool appearing later than
chPool_{n}
, then
o
would
eventually pick it (or some other node with even bigger timestamp), because
m
has either infinite timestamp or finite timestamp but bigger than

maxT_{n}
, which contradicts that
o
did not pick
m
. Therefore, the span of
u
must start after the span of
o
starts.

Lemma 79
.

All the key invariants in Figure
10
hold for the atomic timestamp TS-stack.

Proof B.49
.

Invariant
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}
holds trivially because timestamps are atomic natural numbers, meaning that the
global counter
TS
in Figure
8(a)

increases per push and it is unique to that push, producing only comparable timestamps.

For invariant
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::vis-proof::misses-are-late}}
, suppose
\textsc{Miss}\ {o}\ {u}
.
Hence, we know
o,u\notin E
,
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
(in particular
t_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u}

by the overloading of
<_{\textsc{T}}
), and

\displaystyle\forall o^{\prime}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}

(13)

The result follows by the Misses Lemma
78
if we can show the third
hypothesis in that lemma.

So, let
o^{\prime}\notin E
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}
. We can freely use the structural invariants,
since Lemma
74
already proved them.
Additionally, we can use any lemma of Section
B.1

as long as it
only
uses structural invariants.

Since
o^{\prime}\notin E
, Lemma
27
implies

{u}\sqsubset{o^{\prime}}
. Since
\textit{defined}\,({\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}})
,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pop-ids-imply-span}}
implies

\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})
. Hence, Lemma
51
implies

\mathcal{S}\ {u}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}
, which means
t_{s}\ {u}=t_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}

by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-equal}}
.
In other words,
at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}
, which implies

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}
by (
13
).

And we have the main theorem.

Theorem 80
.

The atomic timestamp TS-stack is linearizable.

Proof B.50
.

By the above lemmas, all the TS-stack invariants hold for the atomic timestamp version. Therefore, by Theorem
73
, the
visibility-style axioms in Figure
3
hold.
Theorem
24

then implies that the atomic timestamp TS-stack is linearizable.

B.2.2
Version: Interval Timestamps

For the interval TS-stack, we define
\ll
over abstract timestamps as,
19
19

19

Recall that we overloaded the symbol
<_{\textsc{T}}

to apply to both timestamps and abstract timestamps in Definition
25
.

\displaystyle t_{2}\ll t_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}t_{2}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{1}\ \vee\ \exists u_{1}\ u_{2}\notin E.\ \textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374ptt_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{1}

\displaystyle\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}u_{1},u_{2}\notin E\ \wedge\ \exists o_{1}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\forall o_{2}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}

First we prove the structural invariants, since they do not depend on
\ll
.
In this way, we can freely use the structural invariants in the rest of the lemmas.

Lemma 81
.

All the structural invariants in Figure
10
hold for the interval timestamp TS-stack.

Proof B.51
.

Identical to Lemma
74
, since
the argument does not depend on the nature of the timestamps: it holds for atomic and
interval timestamps.

Now we prove some basic properties about the algorithm.

Lemma 82
.

In a pool, at any moment, all untaken nodes reachable from the top have strictly decreasing timestamps. In other words,
let
t_{n}
and
t_{m}
be the timestamps of untaken nodes
n
and
m
, respectively. If
m
occurs after
n
when
traversing the linked list starting from the top, then
t_{m}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{n}
.

Proof B.52
.

Identical to the proof of Lemma
76
.

Lemma 83
.

Untaken nodes do not get unlinked. In other words, if
n
is an untaken node that was inserted into a pool, then it remains reachable from the top
of the pool.

Proof B.53
.

Identical to the proof of Lemma
77
.

Lemma 84

(Misses Lemma: Plain Timestamps)
.

If,

•

o,u\notin E
,

•

t_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u}
, and

•

\forall o^{\prime}\notin E.\ \textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}
,

then
{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u}})}
.

Proof B.54
.

The proof is identical to the proof of
Lemma
78
,
since the argument does not depend on the nature of the timestamps: it holds
for atomic and interval timestamps.

Lemma 85

(Misses Lemma: Abstract Timestamps)
.

If,

•

o,u\notin E
,

•

at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
, and

•

\forall o^{\prime}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}
,

then
{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u}})}
.

Proof B.55
.

From hypothesis
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
, we have
t_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u}
.
The result follows by the Misses Lemma: Plain Timestamps
84
if we can show the third
hypothesis in that lemma.

So, let
o^{\prime}\notin E
such that
\textit{id}\ {u}=\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}
. We can freely use the structural invariants,
since Lemma
81
already proved them.
Additionally, we can use any lemma of Section
B.1

as long as it
only
uses structural invariants.

Since
o^{\prime}\notin E
, Lemma
27
implies

{u}\sqsubset{o^{\prime}}
. Since
\textit{defined}\,({\textit{id}\ {o^{\prime}}})
,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pop-ids-imply-span}}
implies

\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})
. Hence, Lemma
51
implies

\mathcal{S}\ {u}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}
, which means
t_{s}\ {u}=t_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}

by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-equal}}
.
In other words,
at_{s}\ {u}=at_{s}\ {o^{\prime}}
, which implies

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{\prime}}})}
by the third hypothesis.

Lemma 86

(Disjoint Generated Timestamps Lemma)
.

If
{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{1}}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}}
, then
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.

Proof B.56
.

Since
{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{1}}}\sqsubset^{S}{\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}}
, the invocations of
newTimestamp
in
Figure
8(b)
were disjoint in time.
Let
[a_{1},b_{1}]
and
[a_{2},b_{2}]
be the intervals generated by
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, respectively.

Notice how the returned intervals at lines
89
,

91
,
93

have as lower bound the first read to the variable
TS
at the start of the procedure
(line
85
). While those intervals always have as
upper bound a value strictly smaller than the value of
TS
at the moment the procedure returns.

This means that
b_{1}<_{\mathbb{N}}TS_{f}<_{\mathbb{N}}TS_{1}<_{\mathbb{N}}\ldots<_{\mathbb{N}}TS_{m}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}a_{2}
,
where
TS_{f}
is the value of the
TS
variable at the moment the call to
newTimestamp
returned for
u_{1}

(
TS_{f}
may also be the result of calls to
newTimestamp
done by other threads),
and
TS_{1}
, …,
TS_{m}
are increases to the global
TS
variable done by other threads.
Therefore,
[a_{1},b_{1}]<_{\textsc{T}}[a_{2},b_{2}]
, and so
(\textit{id}\ {u_{1}},[a_{1},b_{1}])<_{\textsc{T}}(\textit{id}\ {u_{2}},[a_{2},b_{2}])

by definition of the overloading of
<_{\textsc{T}}
for abstract timestamps.

Lemma 87

(Negative Transitivity Lemma: Plain Timestamps)
.

If,

•

\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{1}}})
,
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{2}}})
and
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{3}}})
,

•

t_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{3}}
,

•

u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\notin E
,

•

\textit{id}\ {u_{1}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{1}}
and
\textit{id}\ {u_{2}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{2}}
,

•

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}
,

•

\forall o_{3}\notin E.\ \textit{id}\ {u_{3}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{3}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{3}}})}
.

then,
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{3}}
.

Proof B.57
.

First, we prove the following claim.

Claim: Pops

and

cannot take a node by reaching line

and

We focus on

In particular, the above claim directly implies that pops

cannot prematurely end the scan of the pools
by entering line 26. Instead, the pops are forced to scan all the pools and take a node by reaching line 31.

We now focus on the lemma. Suppose for a contradiction that

Since

Therefore,

Also, since

20

We can use

This means that nodes

and

(because

Additionally, by the 6th hypothesis, nodes

With a similar argument one can conclude that
