is finite
and
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{3}}
by hypothesis. Hence,
maxT_{k_{3}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{i_{3}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{m_{3}}
. This means that while visiting
k_{3}
, variable
maxT
will be set to
t_{m_{3}}
at line 28, which
is a bigger timestamp than
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}
, even though
o_{1}
has to scan pool
k_{1}
still (Contradiction).

–

Case
k_{1}=k_{3}
.

By the claim above, by the time
o_{1}
reaches
k_{1}
, both nodes
i_{1}
and
i_{3}
have been inserted into
k_{1}
.
We must have
i_{1}\neq i_{3}
because
i_{1}
’s timestamp is smaller than
i_{3}
’s by hypothesis (and also,
i_{3}
’s timestamp could be infinite
at this moment). Since
i_{3}
’s timestamp is bigger than
i_{1}
’s, the
i_{3}
node must appear earlier in the pool than
i_{1}
, because
nodes in a pool have strictly decreasing timestamps starting from the top (Lemma
82
).
Hence, the found node at lines 22 and 24 will be either

i_{3}
or some node with even bigger timestamp, contradicting that
o_{1}
took
i_{1}
.

•

Case
k_{2}<k_{3}
.

We claim that by the time
o_{1}
finishes visiting pool
k_{2}
, node
i_{3}
is already present in pool
k_{3}
.

Since
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, then
\mathcal{S}\ {u_{1}}\not\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}
,
otherwise
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
by Lemma
86
.
Therefore,
start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{1}}})
.

Since
start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{1}}})
and
i_{2}
is taken after
i_{1}
, we have that
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are present and remain untaken during the entire last loop of
o_{1}
.
In particular,
k_{2}
is not empty when
o_{1}
visits
k_{2}
.

Denote by
i_{m}
the node assigned to variable
n
and by
t_{m}
the timestamp read at line 24. Denote by
c_{2}
the rep event at line 24.
Denote by
t_{2}^{\prime}
the timestamp that
i_{2}
had when
c_{2}
executed (i.e., either
t_{2}^{\prime}=\infty
or

t_{2}^{\prime}=t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
). We have
t_{2}^{\prime}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{m}
because nodes in the same pool have strictly
decreasing timestamps (Lemma
82
),
and the equality is needed because
i_{2}
could be the found node.

While visiting the
k_{2}
pool, the current value of the
maxT
variable for
o_{1}

must be finite, because the maximum timestamp that
maxT
can take for
o_{1}
is
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}

(i.e., the timestamp of the node taken by
o_{1}
). Therefore, if
t_{2}^{\prime}=\infty
, then
t_{m}=\infty
,
which means that
maxT
will be assigned
t_{m}=\infty
, contradicting that the maximum timestamp that
maxT
is ever
assigned is
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}\infty
.
So, it must be the case that
t_{2}^{\prime}=t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, meaning that

i_{2}
has already been assigned a finite timestamp, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}
has finished before
c_{2}
.

But
start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{3}}})\leq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u_{2}}})
, meaning that
i_{3}
has already been inserted into pool
k_{3}
before
c_{2}
executed.
In addition,
i_{3}
remains untaken during
o_{1}
’s loop because of the 6th hypothesis.
This proves the claim.

Now, either
k_{1}<k_{3}
or
k_{3}<k_{1}
or
k_{1}=k_{3}
. The rest of the proof is identical to the cases for

k_{1}<k_{3}
and
k_{3}<k_{1}
and
k_{1}=k_{3}
in the previous case (i.e., case for
k_{3}<k_{2}
), but using pool
k_{2}

whenever there is a reference to pool
k_{j}
in the cases
k_{1}<k_{3}
,
k_{3}<k_{1}
,
k_{1}=k_{3}
.

Lemma 88

(Negative Transitivity Lemma: Abstract Timestamps)
.

If,

•

\textit{defined}\,({at_{s}\ {u_{1}}})
,
\textit{defined}\,({at_{s}\ {u_{2}}})
and
\textit{defined}\,({at_{s}\ {u_{3}}})
,

•

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{3}}
,

•

u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\notin E
,

•

at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}}
and
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
,

•

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}
,

•

\forall o_{3}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u_{3}}=at_{s}\ {o_{3}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{3}}})}
.

then,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{3}}
.

Proof B.58
.

From the first hypothesis, we obtain
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{1}}})
,
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{2}}})
and
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {u_{3}}})
.

From the second hypothesis,
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{3}}
by overloading of
<_{\textsc{T}}
.

From the fourth hypothesis,
\textit{id}\ {u_{1}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{1}}
and
\textit{id}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
by definition.

So, from Lemma
87
, we would obtain
t_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{3}}
, and hence
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{3}}
if we can show the sixth hypothesis of Lemma
87
.

So, let
o_{3}\notin E
such that
\textit{id}\ {u_{3}}=\textit{id}\ {o_{3}}
. We can freely use the structural invariants,
since Lemma
81
already proved them.
Additionally, we can use any lemma of Section
B.1

as long as it
only
uses structural invariants.

Since
o_{3}\notin E
, Lemma
27
implies

{u_{3}}\sqsubset{o_{3}}
. Since
\textit{defined}\,({\textit{id}\ {o_{3}}})
,
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::pop-ids-imply-span}}
implies

\textit{defined}\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{3}}})
. Hence, Lemma
51
implies

\mathcal{S}\ {u_{3}}\sqsubset^{S}\mathcal{S}\ {o_{3}}
, which means
t_{s}\ {u_{3}}=t_{s}\ {o_{3}}

by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::timestamps-equal}}
.
In other words,
at_{s}\ {u_{3}}=at_{s}\ {o_{3}}
, which implies

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{3}}})}
by the sixth hypothesis.

And now the main lemmas.

Lemma 89
.

\ll
is a strict partial order.

Proof B.59
.

By Lemma
26
,
21
21

21

Note that
the proof of Lemma
26

does not make use of any invariants, because the result
follows directly from definitions. Therefore, we can use
Lemma
26
safely in our proofs.

<_{\textsc{T}}
is still a strict partial order when it is extended to abstract timestamps.

Also, we can freely use the structural invariants,
since Lemma
81
already proved them.

We now show that
\ll
is a strict partial order.

•

Irreflexivity.

Suppose
t\ll t
. Since
t<_{\textsc{T}}t
cannot hold, we must have
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ t\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t
for some
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
Hence,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{s}\ {u_{2}}
. But
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, so
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, which implies
\textit{id}\ {u_{1}}=\textit{id}\ {u_{2}}
by definition of
at_{s}
.
But then,
u_{1}=u_{2}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
, and
from
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
(equivalently,
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{1}}
) we obtain
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}}})}
for some
pop
o_{1}
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}}
(Contradiction).

•

Transitivity.

From
t_{1}\ll t_{2}
and
t_{2}\ll t_{3}
, we need to consider four cases,

–

Case
t_{1}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}
and
t_{2}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
. Hence
t_{1}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
,
meaning
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
by definition.

–

Case
t_{1}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}
and
\exists u^{h_{2}},u^{l_{2}}\notin E.\ \textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ t_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.

Hence,
t_{1}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
.

In other words,
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ t_{1}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
and
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.
So,
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
by definition.

–

Case
\exists u^{h_{1}},u^{l_{1}}\notin E.\ \textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ t_{1}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}
and
t_{2}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.

Hence,
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}<_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.

In other words,
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ t_{1}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.
So,
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
by definition.

–

Case
\exists u^{h_{1}},u^{l_{1}}\notin E.\ \textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ t_{1}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}
and

\exists u^{h_{2}},u^{l_{2}}\notin E.\ \textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ t_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
.

Either
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}
or not.
If
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}
holds, then
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}

and so
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
by definition.

Therefore, we can assume
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}
the rest of the proof.

Claim:
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}
. Suppose
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}
, then
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
, which contradicts the hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
. This proves the claim.

Since
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
and
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
, there are pops
o^{h_{1}}
,
o^{h_{2}}
such that the following facts
hold,

1.

u^{h_{1}},u^{h_{2}},u^{l_{1}},u^{l_{2}},o^{h_{1}},o^{h_{2}}\notin E
.

2.

at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{h_{1}}}
and
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{h_{2}}}
.

3.

\forall o^{l_{1}}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{l_{1}}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{l_{1}}}})}
.

4.

\forall o^{l_{2}}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{l_{2}}}\implies{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{l_{2}}}})}
.

Now, we have
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{2}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
, i.e.,

at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
. So, we have two cases,

*

Case
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
.

Claim:
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})\not<end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})
. If
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})}
, then by Lemma
88
(Negative Transitivity Lemma)
with
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
and
facts 1,2,4 above, we obtain

at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
(contradicts
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
).
This proves the claim.

Claim:
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})\neq end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})
. If
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})=end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})
, then
o^{h_{1}}=o^{h_{2}}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::reps-are-injective}}

and
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}
.
But from fact 2 above,
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{h_{2}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{h_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
, which contradicts the hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
.
This proves the claim.

So, by the above claims,
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})}
. And from fact 3 above,

\displaystyle\forall o^{l_{1}}\notin E.\ at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{l_{1}}}\implies end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})<end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})<end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{l_{1}}}})

(15)

which implies
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
by definition (we already know from facts 1,2 that
u^{h_{2}}\notin E
is taken by

o^{h_{2}}\notin E
).

By Lemma
88
(Negative Transitivity Lemma)
with
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
, facts 1,2 above,

{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})}
, and
formula (
15
), we obtain

at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
.

Hence,
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
holds by definition, because
t_{1}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
and
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}t_{3}
are hypotheses,
and we have
\textsc{Tb}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
and
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{2}}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u^{l_{1}}}
.

*

Case
at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}
.

From facts 2,4 we obtain
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{2}}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o^{h_{1}}}})}
,
since
at_{s}\ {u^{l_{2}}}=at_{s}\ {u^{h_{1}}}=at_{s}\ {o^{h_{1}}}
.

But from fact 3 we get (
15
)
again, and we will obtain
t_{1}\ll t_{3}
by following the steps after formula
(
15
).

Lemma 90
.

\ll
is pop-total.

Proof B.60
.

If
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
holds, then we are done, since
\ll
contains
<_{\textsc{T}}
.
Similarly if
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
. Hence, we can assume
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}

and
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
in the rest of the proof.

We will focus on the case
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o}
, since the other case
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o}
is similar.

Either there is
o_{2}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
or not.

If not, then
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
holds trivially.
Also, since
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
holds trivially, we have
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
by definition.

Hence, we can assume that there is a pop
o_{2}\notin E
such that
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
.
Since
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {o}})
and
\textit{defined}\,({t_{s}\ {o_{2}}})
, both pops executed their spans by definition of
t_{s}
.

•

Case
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}
.
We claim that
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
. We already know
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o}
.
Let
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}^{\prime}}
for some
o_{2}^{\prime}\notin E
.
But
at_{s}\ {o_{2}^{\prime}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
, meaning

o_{2}=o_{2}^{\prime}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
.
And from the case hypothesis,
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}={end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}^{\prime}}})}
holds.
Thus
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
.

Finally, since
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
holds trivially,
and
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, we have
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
by definition.

•

Case
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}
.

We claim that
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{2}}\ {u_{1}}
. We already know
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}
.
Let
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o_{1}^{\prime}}
for some
o_{1}^{\prime}\notin E
. But
at_{s}\ {o_{1}^{\prime}}=at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o}
, meaning

o=o_{1}^{\prime}
by
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::prop-funcs-are-injective}}
.
And from the case hypothesis,
{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})}<{end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}={end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{1}^{\prime}}})}
holds.
Thus
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{2}}\ {u_{1}}
.

Finally, since
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ \wedge\ at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
holds trivially,
and
at_{s}\ {u_{2}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
, we have
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
by definition.

•

Case
end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})=end\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o_{2}}})
. From
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::reps-are-injective}}

and
I_{\ref{inv::stack::appendix::vis-proof::spans-are-injective}}
,
o=o_{2}
.
Hence,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {o}=at_{s}\ {o_{2}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
. Therefore,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}=at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
.

Lemma 91
.

Invariant
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::appendix::vis-proof::disjoint-push}}
holds.

Proof B.61
.

By Lemma
86
,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
holds.
Hence,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\ll at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
by definition.

Lemma 92
.

Invariant
I_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::vis-proof::misses-are-late}}
holds.

Proof B.62
.

From the hypothesis
\textsc{Miss}\ {o}\ {u}
, we have
at_{s}\ {o}\ll at_{s}\ {u}
. Hence, there are two cases,

•

Case
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
. The result follows by Lemma
85
and hypothesis
\textsc{Miss}\ {o}\ {u}
.

•

Case there are
u_{1}
,
u_{2}\notin E
, such that
\textsc{Tb}\ {u_{1}}\ {u_{2}}
,
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
,
at_{s}\ {o}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
, and
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
.

Claim:
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {o}
. If
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {o}
, then
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {o}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}

(Contradicts case hypothesis
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{2}}
). This proves the claim.

Either
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
or not.

If
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
, then
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\leq_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
. From Lemma

and hypothesis
\textsc{Miss}\ {o}\ {u}
, we obtain
{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u}})}
.

Therefore, we can assume
at_{s}\ {o}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
in the rest of the proof.

Either
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
or not.

If
at_{s}\ {o}<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
, then Lemma

and hypothesis
\textsc{Miss}\ {o}\ {u}

imply
{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {o}})}<{start\,({\mathcal{S}\ {u}})}
.

So, we can assume
at_{s}\ {o}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
in the rest of the proof.

In other words, we have
at_{s}\ {u_{1}}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {o}
,
at_{s}\ {o}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u_{1}}
and
at_{s}\ {o}\not<_{\textsc{T}}at_{s}\ {u}
.
We now show that this leads to a contradiction (meaning that this case is impossible).

From

.

Appendix C
Visibility and Separability for Other Data Structures

In this section we show how the methodology
laid out in Section
3.2

can be used to provide visibility-style concurrent axioms
for other data structures. We exemplify with
RDCSS and MCAS (Section
C.1
),
queues (Section
C.2
),
and locks (Section
C.3
).

For RDCSS and MCAS we provide a detailed
explanation of how their visibility-style axioms
are derived and how their visibility and separability
relations are defined. For queues and locks we will
only state the sequential specifications and the result
of transforming it by following the methodology.

C.1
RDCSS and MCAS

We focus on
Restricted Double-Compare Single Swap (RDCSS)
and

Multiple Compare-And-Swap (MCAS)
as presented in Harris
et al.

[
7
]
.

C.1.1
The algorithms

The RDCSS algorithm

RDCSS is
a generalization of the compare-and-swap operation CAS. Whereas

\textit{CAS}\,(pt_{2},exp_{2},new_{2})
updates
pt_{2}
with
new_{2}
if the
old value of
pt_{2}
is
exp_{2}
, RDCSS adds another pointer
pt_{1}
and
value
exp_{1}
into the decision. More precisely, RDCSS receives as
input a descriptor that is a record with five pieces of data: two
pointers
pt_{1}
,
pt_{2}
and three values
exp_{1}
,
exp_{2}
,
new_{2}
.
To the invoking client, RDCSS gives the impression that it

atomically
carries out the following update: if
pt_{1}
has
expected value
exp_{1}
and
pt_{2}
has expected value
exp_{2}
, then

pt_{2}
is updated to
new_{2}
.

record

Figure 11
:
Pseudo code of the RDCSS implementation.
!
,

Alloc
and
CAS
are the system calls for memory
dereference, allocation, and compare-and-swap.

Figure
11
shows the
implementation of the RDCSS data structure (
rdcss
and associated
methods, and the descriptor type
rdesc
).
Val
denotes the
set of all possible input values; it excludes descriptors and
descriptor-storing pointers.
Every pointer exposed to the clients is classified as a control
(
cptr
) or data (
dptr
) pointer. Data pointers may
store
Val
values, descriptors, and pointers, while control
pointers only store
Val
values. Along with the
rdcss

method, the implementation exports data pointer methods

rread
,
rwrite
, and
rCAS
. The latter
replace the system calls for pointer dereference, update, and

CAS
, which they adapt for data
pointers. Also exported, are the methods for
dereference, update and
CAS
over control pointers,

rread-c
,
rwrite-c
, and
rCAS-c
,
respectively,
implemented simply as respective system calls.
Methods
\textit{rdcss}^{\prime}

and
complete
are internal, and not exported.
The implementation further provides: (1) an exported method

\textit{ralloc}\,(v,t)
for allocating a pointer of type

t\in\{\textsc{cptr},\textsc{dptr}\}
with initial value
v
, which replaces
the system operation
Alloc
, and (2) an internal Boolean
predicate
\textit{is\_rdesc}\,(p)
that returns true iff pointer
p

stores an RDCSS descriptor.
Details for the allocation of data and control pointers are
left unspecified.
For example, Harris
et al.
[
7
]
suggests that
data and control pointers can be allocated in different areas
of the memory. Similarly, details for the implementation of

is_rdesc
are left unspecified. One possibility
for implementing
is_rdesc
suggested
by Harris
et al.
, is that one bit could be reserved in the pointers
to indicate if the pointer stores a descriptor or not,
so that
\textit{is\_rdesc}(p)
simply reads the reserved bit
in
p
.

We next give a high-level description of
rdcss
. Any thread
T

invoking
rdcss
allocates a fresh address
d
for the input
descriptor
desc
(line
7
);
d
will
serve as a unique identifier for the
rdcss
invocation. Next,
T

calls the recursive procedure
\textit{rdcss}^{\prime}
. This first CASs on

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
to read the
old
value and write

d
if the old value is the expected
{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}

(line
11
). By this write,
T
essentially requests
help with
d
, and signals to other threads that:

•

Descriptor
desc
(which is stored in
d
) is currently
active in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
.

•

T
is invoking
rdcss
in which pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{1}
still needs to be read.

•

While
desc
remains active in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
, value

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
is stored in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}

indirectly
, as it can be reached by following the descriptor.

After the CAS in line
11
, if
old
is a
descriptor, then
T
first
helps
by invoking

\textit{complete}{(\textit{old})}

(line
13
), and then
T
recurses to
reattempt its task. If
old
is a value not matching

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
, then no modification to

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
is performed and
T

returns
old
. If
old
is the expected value (i.e.,
the CAS succeeded), then
T
invokes
\textit{complete}{(d)}
to help itself
(line
17
).

We now describe
complete
from the point of view of another thread

T^{\prime}
that reads
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
and finds
d
(lines

12
,
30
,

37
,
45
). Before
doing anything else,
T^{\prime}
helps on
d
by invoking
complete
as
follows (lines
13
,

31
,

38
,

46
).
T^{\prime}
first reads pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{1}
(line
22
). If it
finds the expected value
{\textit{desc}}.exp_{1}
, and since pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
indirectly stores the expected value, then

rdcss
can succeed; thus,
T^{\prime}
attempts to replace the descriptor
in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
with the new value

{\textit{desc}}.new_{2}
(line
24
). The
replacement in line
24
is performed by

CAS
, which may fail if some other thread managed to help
T

before
T^{\prime}
. Either way,
T
has been helped after the call to

complete
.

Otherwise, if
T^{\prime}
does not find the expected value

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{1}
, it attempts to undo
T
’s modification to

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
(line
26
) by
replacing
d
in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
back to

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
, via
CAS
. This undoing may fail if
some other thread managed to help
T
before
T^{\prime}
. Either way,
T

has again been helped after the call to
complete
.

Procedures
rread
,
rCAS
and
rwrite
follow
the same helping strategy, i.e., whenever they find a descriptor in
their input pointer, they invoke
complete
to help the pending

rdcss
before recursing to reattempt their own task.
Procedures for control pointers
rread-c
,

rCAS-c
and
rwrite-c

directly execute their respective system calls, because
no helping is required for control pointers, as
descriptors cannot be stored in them.

The MCAS algorithm

MCAS also generalizes
CAS, but it updates an arbitrary number of pointers at once.
More precisely, MCAS receives a list of update entries, each being a
record with three pieces of data: a pointer
pt
and two
values
exp
,
new
. To a client, MCAS gives the
impression that it atomically carries out the following conditional
multiple update: if for every update entry
i
, the pointer

{\textit{pt}}_{i}
has expected value

{\textit{exp}}_{i}
, then for every update entry
j
, the
pointer
{\textit{pt}}_{j}
is updated to the new value

{\textit{new}}_{j}
.

\textit{is\_mdesc}\,(\textit{old})

then

53:

if

\textit{old}\neq d

then

54:

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}\,(\textit{old})

55:

return
\textit{writeall}\,(d,\textit{desc})

56:

else

if

\textit{old}\neq{e}.exp

then

57:

return
Fail

58:

return
Succ

Figure 12
:
Pseudo code of the MCAS implementation. It makes use of the exportable
procedures in the RDCSS implementation of Figure
11
.

Figure
12
shows the implementation of the MCAS data structure
(
mcas
and associated methods, and the descriptor type

mdesc
) atop RDCSS.

Val
is the set of input values that excludes descriptors
and descriptor-storing pointers.
We use the notation
{pt}_{i}
,
{exp}_{i}
, and

{new}_{i}
to refer to the components of an
update_entry

i
.

Along with the

mcas
method, the implementation exports data pointer methods
mread
,

mwrite
, and
mCAS
.
The latter adapt the RDCSS data pointer operations to handle the use of MCAS descriptors.
Procedures
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
and
writeall
are internal and not exported.

The implementation further provides: (1) an exported method

\textit{malloc}\,(v,t)
for allocating a data pointer with initial
value
v
, which replaces
the RDCSS operation
ralloc
, and (2) an internal Boolean
predicate
\textit{is\_mdesc}\,(p)
that returns true iff pointer
p

stores an MCAS descriptor.
Details for the implementation of

is_mdesc
are left unspecified. One possibility
for implementing
is_mdesc
suggested
by Harris
et al.
, is that one bit could be reserved in the pointers
to indicate if the pointer stores an MCAS descriptor or not,
so that
\textit{is\_mdesc}(p)
simply reads the reserved bit
in
p
.

A high-level description of
mcas
is as follows. Thread
T

invoking
mcas
first creates an MCAS descriptor
desc

containing the non-empty list of update entries and a status pointer
(line
31
). The status pointer starts in an
undecided state (
Undec
) and evolves into either a success
(
Succ
) or failed (
Fail
) state.
Succ
indicates
that the descriptor was written into all input pointers, while

Fail
indicates the failure of at least one such write. Next,
T

allocates a fresh address
d
for
desc

(line
32
), which serves as the unique identifier
for the
mcas
invocation. Eventually, through a call to

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
,
T
invokes
writeall

(line
39
) to attempt storing
d
into each
input pointer, via
rdcss
. If any individual
rdcss
of

writeall
fails, then
T
attempts to mark the status
pointer of
desc
as failed (line
40
). In
general,
T
writing
d
into pointer
{pt}_{i}
of entry
i

signals that writing
d
into previous entries has succeeded and:

•

Descriptor
desc
(stored in
d
) is currently active
in
{pt}_{j}
for every
j\leq i
.

•

T
has an ongoing
mcas
in which pointers

{pt}_{j}
for
j>i
still need to be updated with
d
.

•

Pointers
{pt}_{j}
for
j\leq i
indirectly have
their expected values.

If another thread
T^{\prime}
attempts to access
{pt}_{i}
and finds

d
, it will
help

T
complete
mcas
before doing anything
else, by invoking
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
as follows.
T^{\prime}
first attempts to
write the descriptor into all remaining pointers
(line
39
). If
T^{\prime}
succeeds, then all
pointers had the expected values. The variable
s
in line

39
is set to

Succ
, and
T^{\prime}
attempts changing the status field of
desc

accordingly (line
40
) and replacing all the
descriptors with the new values (line
44
). These
attempts utilize
CAS
which may fail if some other thread
already helped
T
to either succeed or fail in its
mcas
.
Alternatively, if
T^{\prime}
fails to write
d
into some pointer, it means
that not all pointers had the expected values. The variable
s
in
line
39
is bound to
Fail
,
T^{\prime}
attempts
to change the status field of
desc
accordingly
(line
40
), and to undo
T
’s writing of
d

(line
44
). As
before, the
CAS
’s in these attempts may fail if some other
thread already helped
T
to either succeed or fail in its
mcas
.

Notice that
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
and
writeall
are mutually
recursive. This is necessary, because while a thread
T
is writing
the descriptor into all the input pointers using
writeall
,

T
may encounter other descriptors that force it to help by invoking

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
(lines

52
-
54
),
after which
T
must reattempt writing the descriptor again
(line
55
).

Procedures
mread
,
mCAS
and
mwrite

follow the same helping strategy as the procedures

rread
,
rCAS
and
rwrite
for RDCSS in
Figure
11
, i.e., whenever they find an MCAS descriptor in
their input pointer, they invoke
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
to help the pending

mcas
before recursing to reattempt their own task.

Sequential Specification

Figure
13
shows the
sequential state-based and history-based specifications for

rdcss
.

The state-based specification in
Figure
13(a)
says in axiom
A_{1}
that a
successful
\textit{rdcss}(d)
occurs when input pointers
{d}.pt_{1}

and
{d}.pt_{2}
contain the
expected values; pointer
{d}.pt_{2}
is then mutated to its new
value and
rdcss
returns the expected value for pointer

{d}.pt_{2}
. Axioms
A_{2.1}
and
A_{2.2}
state the failing cases for

rdcss
, i.e., the cases in which
rdcss
leaves the heap
unchanged.
For
A_{2.1}
,
rdcss
fails if pointer
{d}.pt_{2}
has the
expected value, but pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
does not.
For
A_{2.2}
,
rdcss
fails if pointer
{d}.pt_{2}
does not
have the expected value. Note that in
A_{2.2}
,
rdcss
is
not required to read pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
, but both axioms

A_{2.1}
and
A_{2.2}
imply that
rdcss
always reads pointer

{d}.pt_{2}
.

Figure
13(b)
shows the history sequential
specification for
rdcss
. The specification utilizes the

visibility relation

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
to
capture a read-write causal dependence between events. In particular,

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}
means that “event
r
reads a value
that event
w
wrote into pointer
p
”.
Under this interpretation,
axioms
B_{1},...,B_{4.2}
state the following
expected properties.

Axiom
B_{1}
(No in-between) says that if
r
reads from
w
in
p
,
then no other successful
p
-write can occur between
w
and
r

(otherwise, such a write would overwrite
w
). Again,

\sqsubset
denotes the
returns-before
relation (with

\sqsubseteq
its reflexive closure), where

{x}\sqsubset{y}
means that
x
terminated before
y
started.

Set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
collects the successful
p
-writes, e.g.,
writes of the form
\textit{rwrite}(p,\_)
and
\textit{rwrite-c}(p,\_)
,
CASes of the form
\textit{rCAS}(p,e,\_)
and
\textit{rCAS-c}(p,e,\_)

returning the expected value
e
(hence, the CASes succeed),

ralloc
events
returning
p
(hence, they allocate
p
and write in
p

some initial value), and
\textit{rdcss}(d)
events returning
the expected value for pointer
p={d}.pt_{2}
.

rdcss
has the peculiarity that it is not possible to know if it succeeded
even when it returns the expected value for
{d}.pt_{2}

(meaning that
rdcss
read the expected value
in
{d}.pt_{2}
), because the output does not say anything
about the value in the first pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
.
This is illustrated by Axioms
A_{1}
and

A_{2.1}
, where in both cases
\textit{rdcss}(d)
returns
{d}.exp_{2}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{W}_{p}
adds another clause for
rdcss
regarding
pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
, which states that
\textit{rdcss}(d)
observes
a writer having in its input the expected value for
{d}.pt_{1}
.
We now explain the predicate
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
.

Predicate
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
expresses that
x

is a writer event (independently if it succeeds to write or not)
intending to write input
v
in pointer
p
.
It collects events of the form

\textit{rwrite}(p,v)
and
\textit{rwrite-c}(p,v)
,
CASes of the form
\textit{rCAS}(p,\_,v)
and
\textit{rCAS-c}(p,\_,v)
,
allocs of the form
\textit{ralloc}(v)
returning pointer
p

(so that
p
gets initialized with
v
),
and
rdcss
events having
{d}.new_{2}=v
for pointer

p={d}.pt_{2}
.
In the case of
rdcss
, definition
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}

does not treat
rdcss
as a writer of
{d}.pt_{1}
,
because
rdcss
never writes into
pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
.

Intuitively, one should think of the statement

x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

as expressing that
x
actually modified the heap
by writing
some
value in
p
, as in this diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H}[{{p}\mapsto{\_}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

If we know the more precise statement
x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
,
then
x
modified the heap
by writing
v
in
p
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

Axiom
B_{2}
(Observed events are writes) says that any observed event
at
p
must be a successful
p
-write, i.e., if
r
reads from
w
in

p
, then
w
must have actually written into
p
. Axiom
B_{3}

(Dependences occur in the past) says that if a read depends on a
write, then the write executes before the read.

Axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
express what is true
about the output of an
rdcss
.
Since it is not possible to know if
rdcss
succeeds or not
just by looking at its output, axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}

encode indirectly the successful and failing cases in the
state-based sequential specification for
rdcss
as follows.

Axiom
B_{4.1}
states that
\textit{rdcss}(d)
always
reads pointer
{d}.pt_{2}
, i.e.,
\textit{rdcss}(d)

observes an event
w_{2}
at
{d}.pt_{2}
. Event
w_{2}
executes before

\textit{rdcss}(d)
due to axiom
B_{3}
,
w_{2}
actually writes
v

due to axiom
B_{2}
, and pointer
{d}.pt_{2}

does not mutate after
w_{2}
and before
\textit{rdcss}(d)

due to axiom
B_{1}
. Diagrammatically,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{rdcss}(d)\left<v\right>}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d}.pt_{2}\mapsto v\text{ persists}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}}

If
v\neq{d}.exp_{2}
, then
\textit{rdcss}(d)\notin\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}
by definition,
and so
rdcss
fails by not mutating the heap as in axiom
A_{2.2}
.

If
v={d}.exp_{2}
, then Axiom
B_{4.2}
states that
\textit{rdcss}(d)
additionally
reads
{d}.pt_{1}
by observing some event
w_{1}
. By a similar argument,

w_{1}
will mutate the heap by writing some value
v^{\prime}
in
{d}.pt_{1}
that
persists up to the execution of
\textit{rdcss}(d)
.

If
v^{\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{1}
, then
\textit{rdcss}(d)\notin\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}

by definition, and so
rdcss
fails by not mutating the heap as in
axiom
A_{2.1}
.

If instead
v^{\prime}={d}.exp_{1}
, then
\textit{rdcss}(d)\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}

by definition, and since we also have

\textsc{writer}\ {\textit{rdcss}(d)\left<\_\right>}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.new_{2}}
by definition,

rdcss
actually mutates
{d}.pt_{2}
as in Axiom
A_{1}
.

(

) No in-between

({w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}\wedge w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{{p}})\implies({w^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w}\vee{r}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w^{\prime}})

(a)

Concurrent specification. Relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
are
existentially quantified.

Constraint relation

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\bigcup_{p}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}\cup\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

{e}\sqsubset{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{e}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{e^{\prime}}.\textit{start}

Closure of terminated events

\overline{T}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\mid\exists t\in T.\ e\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}t\}

\mathcal{Q}_{{\textit{rdcss}(d)},{v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{aligned} \exists w_{2}.\,{w_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\textit{rdcss}(d)}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {w_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v}\wedge{}\\

Defined notions.

Figure 14
:
Concurrent history-based specification for
rdcss
. Variables

,

,

,

,

range over
\overline{T}
.

,

range over

.

Concurrent Specification

As was explained in Section
3.2
,
concurrent execution histories do not satisfy sequential history-based specifications
for two main reasons. First,
concurrent events can
overlap
in real time. As a consequence,
the axioms
B_{1}
(No in-between) and
B_{3}
(Dependencies occur in the
past) are too restrictive, as they force events to be disjoint, due to
the use of the returns-before relation
\sqsubset
.
Second, events can no longer be treated as atomic; thus event’s start
and end times (if the event is terminated) must be taken into
account. As a consequence, axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
and set

\mathcal{W}_{p}
must be modified to account for the output of an
unfinished event not being available yet.

Figure
14
shows the concurrent
specification that addresses the above issues. As expected, in
addition to the visibility relation, the specification utilizes the

separability relation

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
.
We now explain how the concurrent specification of
Figure
14
is obtained from the sequential
one in Figure
13(b)
.

Axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between-rdcss}}
is obtained from
B_{1}
by
carrying out the replacement method described in
Section
3.2

where we replace
\sqsubset
with
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
.
23
23

23

Note
that since
\sqsubset
occurs in positive position in
B_{1}
,
the replacement method states that we do not need to flip the order of
the variables nor apply a negation.

The intuition
is that we want to relax the real-time strong separation imposed by

\sqsubset
into a more permissive separation

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
. We shall see in
Section
C.1.4

the concrete definition of

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
for the

rdcss
implementation of Figure
11
.

Axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-observed-are-writes-rdcss}}
is unchanged compared to
B_{2}
.

Axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence-rdcss}}
is obtained from
B_{3}

identically as in Section
3.2

for obtaining
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-no-future-dependence}}
.
Here, the
constraint
relation

\prec
is again the union of the
visibility and separability relations

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{\cup_{p}{(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}\cup\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p})}}
.

Axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion-rdcss}}
has an identical explanation
as that for axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::stack::cc-return-completion}}
in
Section
3.2
.
Axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion-rdcss}}
simply coalesces
axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
into the
postcondition predicate
\mathcal{Q}
.

We further emphasize that in the concurrent setting we also need to
redefine the set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
of successful writes into pointer
p
.
It does not suffice to state that
rdcss

returns
{d}.exp_{2}
; we need a criterion for an unfinished
rdcss
.
Thus, we define in
Figure
14(b)
that an

\textit{rdcss}(d)
is a successful
p
-write if
p={d}.pt_{2}

and
\textit{rdcss}(d)
observes events writing
the expected values for both pointers
{d}.pt_{1}
and

{d}.pt_{2}
.

C.1.3
The specification for MCAS

We will focus on the specification of the
mcas
procedure.
The specification of the full MCAS module, which includes
procedures
mread
,
mwrite
,
and
malloc
, can be found in
Domínguez
et al.

[
4
]
.

(A_{1})
Successful
mcas

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle true\rangle}{H}[{{{pt}_{i}}\mapsto{{new}_{i}}}]_{i\in\overline{u}}

if
\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ H({pt}_{i})={exp}_{i}

(A_{2})
Failing
mcas

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle false\rangle}H

if
\exists i\in\overline{u}.\ H({pt}_{i})\neq{exp}_{i}

(a)

\begin{aligned} {\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}&\ x=\textit{mcas}([\ldots,\{{pt}:p,\ {exp}:\_,\ {new}:v\},\ldots])\left<\_\right>\vee{}\\

Sequential Specification

Figure

shows the

.

The state-based specification in
Figure
15(a)
says in axiom
A_{1}
that a
successful
mcas
occurs when all input pointers contain the
expected values; the pointers are then mutated to their new
values. Axiom
A_{2}
says that a failing
mcas
occurs when some
input pointer does not have the expected value, leaving the heap
unchanged.

Figure

shows the history sequential
specification for
mcas
. Similarly to the specification of

rdcss
, Figure

utilizes the

visibility relation

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
to
capture a read-write causal dependence between events. In particular,

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}
means that “event
r
reads a value
that event
w
wrote into pointer
p
”.
Under this interpretation,
axioms
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, and
B_{3}
are identical to those
for
rdcss
in Figure
13
.
This should not be surprising, since both
rdcss

and
mcas
are read-write pointer procedures,
which means that there must exist some common properties
among them.

Similarly to the specification of
rdcss
,
set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
collects the successful
p
-writes, e.g.,
writes of the form
\textit{mwrite}(p,\_)
,
malloc

returning
p
, and
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
events returning
true
and having
p
in

\overline{u}
.

Axioms

(Successful

) and
B_{4.2}
(Failing

) essentially encode the state-based sequential

. For example, axiom

directly
says that a successful
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\left<true\right>

—a successful write

) that
wrote the expected value into the
appropriate pointer. Because the write events are

by

B_{3}

, while

guarantees the none of them is
overwritten before
r

for each entry

, the execution
looks as follows, where the write into pointer
{pt}_{i}

persists until the heap
H_{n}
at which

Concurrent Specification

Axioms
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
,
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-observed-are-writes}}
,
and
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
are obtained identically as
the corresponding ones in Section
C.1.2
.

Also, axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
has an identical explanation
as in Section
C.1.2
.
The axiom simply coalesces
axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
into the
postcondition predicate
\mathcal{Q}
.

Similarly, we need to redefine the set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
of successful writes into pointer
p
.
It does not suffice to consider an
mcas
as a successful write if
it returns
true
; we need a criterion when an unfinished
mcas

is a successful write as well. Thus, we define in
Figure
16(b)
that an

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
is a successful
p
-write if
p
is a pointer
in
\overline{u}
, and
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
observes events writing
the expected values for each entry in
\overline{u}
.

C.1.4
Defining the Visibility and Separability Relations

Figure 17
:
An execution of
mcas
events
A
and
B
. Each event
encircles the spans belonging to it (i.e., helping it). The
intersection of spans for each event are shown as gray
columns. Spans
a
and
b
, both accessing pointer
p
, are named
for later reference.

Defining the relations for
mcas

We now proceed to explain how relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}
and

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
are defined for the implementation of
mcas
in Figure

12
. We focus first on
mcas
because it illustrates
the most general case, as it turns out that
rdcss
is
a special case of the analysis we do in this section.

The key idea is to analyze the behavior of the descriptors during the
execution. Figure
17
shows the execution of two
overlapping invocations of
mcas
, which we refer to as events
A

and
B
, respectively.
24
24

24

We focus on the successful

mcas
case. Dominguez
et al.

[
4
]
also describe the
failing
mcas
case. Nevertheless, the successful case suffices
to discover the definitions for
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}
and

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
, since the failing case also
produces the same definitions.
During the execution of

mcas
, threads write the descriptor into a pointer
(line
51
in Figure
12
), and then
remove it (line
44
). We term
span
the
time interval between writing the descriptor and removing it. We also
say that the removed descriptor is

resolved
. Figure
17
depicts spans as
bounded line segments.

There are two key invariants of
mcas
that give rise to
its visibility and separability relations.
The first key
invariant is that all the spans of an event
must overlap
(we
call this the
bunching invariant
). Concretely for
mcas

events, any descriptor write must occur before any descriptor
removal. This is so because of the following two properties related to
how control flows through
mcas
. First, threads can reach
line
44
(descriptor removal) only if some
thread changed the descriptor status at
line
40
.
25
25

25

Recall that all descriptors
are
Undec
initially, and can evolve to
Succ
or

Fail
only.
This holds because
line
44
(descriptor removal) is in the

\mathit{false}
branch of the conditional at
line
38
that checks if the descriptor
status is
Undec
. Therefore, if no thread has changed the
descriptor status at line
40
, threads starting
the
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
procedure will keep entering the
\mathsf{true}

branch at line
38
. The second property
is that any descriptor write at line
51
must
occur before some thread changes the descriptor status at
line
40
. This holds because the
rdcss
at
line
51
(descriptor write attempt) fails if
the descriptor status is no longer
Undec
. These two properties
imply that any descriptor write must occur before the status change,
and any descriptor removal must occur after the status change, i.e.,
any span starts before any span finishes in the event; hence, span
intersection is non-empty. Figure
17
depicts the
spans’ intersection for each event as gray columns. Since the presence
of a descriptor signals that a pointer indirectly has the expected
value, inside the gray column all the event’s pointers simultaneously
have the expected values. Therefore, the event can be considered as
abstractly occurring (i.e., having its LP) anywhere inside the gray
column.

The second key invariant is that spans writing into
the same
pointer
(e.g., spans
a
and
b
in
Figure
17
)
must be disjoint
(we call this
the
disjointness invariant
).
This is so because
mcas
uses helping; thus an event writes into
a pointer only if there is no descriptor currently present. More
specifically, the
rdcss
at line
51
in
Figure
12
writes the descriptor only if the expected value
is present directly (i.e., not via a descriptor). Since spans start
by writing a descriptor, no span can start while there is a descriptor present, or
equivalently, when another span is already active in the pointer. The span
can start only after the currently present descriptor is removed.

These two invariants imply, as also apparent from
Figure
17
, that gray columns of
different events
A
and
B
accessing a common pointer must be
disjoint. Thus, we can
separate
event
A
before event
B
in
time, because
A
’s gray column
executes before
B
’s. In the figure, we can describe the separation
(i.e., the gap between the gray columns) by saying that
there is a span in
A
(namely
a
) and a span in
B
(namely
b
),
both accessing a common pointer, and
a
completes before
b
starts.
Spans
a
and
b
, being disjoint, induce a gap in the
gray columns.

We define the separability relation
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
so that
it directly formalizes the above description of the separation between
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle{A}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{B}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\exists x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A),y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B).\ {x}\sqsubset^{S}{y}

(18)

In the definition,
x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A)
means that
x
is a span
accessing pointer
p
in event
A
(similarly for

y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B)
), and
{x}\sqsubset^{S}{y}
means that span
x

terminates before
y
starts.
Relation
\sqsubset^{S}
is a partial order on spans that
totally orders spans accessing the same pointer due to span
disjointness.
\sqsubseteq^{S}
denotes its reflexive closure.

We now focus on the visibility relation

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
. We previously informally explained
that
{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}
intuitively captures that event

B
reads a value written by the
p
-write
A
, with no other
intervening writes between.
To illustrate how this intuition can be expressed using spans, consider the
span
b
in Figure
17
.
When this span starts, it reads the input pointer (this is so because the
rdcss
at
line
51
in Figure
12
obtains the
pointer’s value, writes the descriptor and returns the read
value). This obtained value must have been written by the most recent
span that successfully wrote into the pointer, which, in
Figure
17
is span
a
.

To formally capture the described situation, we say that event
A
is

observed
by
B
at pointer
p
if there are spans
x
and
y
in

A
and
B
, respectively, such that
x
is the most recent span that terminated before

y
started, and that successfully wrote into
p
,

\displaystyle{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\exists x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A),y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B).\ x=\max_{\sqsubseteq^{S}}\{z\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}\mid{z}\sqsubset^{S}{y}\}

(19)

In the definition, the maximum is taken under

\sqsubseteq^{S}
, and set
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
collects the
spans that
successfully
write into
p
. We differentiate
successful writes because some spans do not carry out changes. For
example, line
44
in Figure

12
produces a span that writes the new value only if the
descriptor’s status was set to
Succ
, but produces a span that
undoes changes (as if the descriptor was not written) if the status
was set to
Fail
.
Notice that it makes sense
to take the maximum under
\sqsubseteq^{S}

because spans accessing the same pointer are disjoint, hence,
linearly ordered under
\sqsubseteq^{S}
.

Spans and the Visibility-Style Axioms

We
briefly illustrate how the definitions satisfy the no in-between axiom

H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
using Figure
17
. In
Figure
17
, assertion

{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}
means that span
b
reads a value
written by span
a
in pointer
p
. Since
a
is the most recent

p
-writing span before
b
in real-time, and due to span
disjointness, any other
p
-writing span
c
must execute either
completely before
a
or completely after
b
. Therefore, if
c

belongs to some
p
-write
C
, there will be a gap between the gray
columns of either
C
and
A
or
B
and
C
, depending on whether
c

executes before
a
or after
b
, meaning that either

{C}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{A}
or
{B}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{C}
.

Alternative Span Definitions

As we just saw, the definitions
(
18
) and
(
19
) satisfy axiom
H_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
.
Notice, however, that the argument relied solely on the span disjointness
invariant, and not on a specific definition of spans. This is actually
an overarching property that we shall utilize when proving each of the
visibility axioms from Figure
16(a)
.
Neither proof will depend on the particular definition of spans, but
only on high-level abstract span invariants such as bunching,
disjointness and a small number of additional structural
ones
[
4
]
.

This abstraction affords some freedom to deviate from the operational
definition of spans as the time intervals between writing the descriptor
and resolving it,
so long as the high-level span invariants remain satisfied by the
programs.

To illustrate, bunching and disjointness remain satisfied if the
successful CAS at line
40
is chosen as the
ending point of a span, instead of line
44

that we previously considered. Recall that while explaining the bunching
invariant,
we stated that any descriptor write must occur before the status change
at line
40
. Therefore, the successful CAS at line
40
is perfectly
fine to end spans, because any span must start before it, i.e., bunching
holds because any span starts before the CAS, which is the ending point for all the spans.
Disjointness still holds because of the same reason: no descriptor can be
written if there is currently a descriptor present. Therefore, if we end the span
prematurely at line
40
, still no span can start until the descriptor
is removed at line
44
much later.

Additionally, single reads that return a
Val
value can be treated as
spans that write a descriptor and instantaneously resolve it (i.e. a “collapsed span”).
For example, the read at line
10
.
It is essential that we treat as spans only those reads that return
Val
values
to ensure the span disjointness invariant: if we treat reads that
return descriptors as spans, then the read will occur while a descriptor is present,
i.e., inside another span, violating disjointness.

Defining the relations for
rdcss

Perhaps surprisingly, definitions
(
18
) and
(
19
) apply without change
to
rdcss
as well.

The reason is that
rdcss
events can be seen as a special case of
Figure
17
, in which each
rdcss
event
executes at most two spans. The first span of
rdcss
is generated
by writing the descriptor at line
11
in Figure

11
, and resolved at either line

24
or line

26
. The second span consists of a single
read of the control pointer at line
22
(thus, it
is a “collapsed” span). Trivially,
rdcss
satisfies the bunching
invariant because the thread that resolves the descriptor at either
line
24
or line

26
, must have also previously read the
control pointer at line
22
while the descriptor
was present. In other words, the collapsed span overlaps the first
span. Also,
rdcss
satisfies span disjointness: an
rdcss

invocation cannot write a new descriptor into a pointer if there is
currently another descriptor present.

A full proof that these definitions satisfy the visibility-style axioms
of Figures
14
and

16
can be found in
Dominguez
et al.

[
4
]
.

C.2
Queues

(A_{1})
Non-empty
deq

{v}::{Q}\xrightarrow{deq()\ \langle v\rangle}Q

(A_{2})
Empty
deq

[\,]\xrightarrow{deq()\ \langle\textsc{EMPTY}\rangle}[\,]

(A_{3})

enq

Q\xrightarrow{enq(v)\ \langle tt\rangle}Q\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}[{v}]

(a)

State-based sequential specification. The queue is represented as a list, with
head the first element of the list.
\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}
denotes list concatenation.

(B_{1})
FIFO

{e_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge{e_{2}}\sqsubset{e_{1}}\implies\exists d_{2}.\ {e_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\wedge{d_{2}}\sqsubset{d_{1}}

(B_{2})
Dequeue uniqueness

{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\implies d_{1}=d_{2}

(B_{3})
Dependences occur in the past

{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}\implies{e}\sqsubset{d}

(B_{4.1})
Non-empty
deq

d=deq()\left<v\right>\wedge v\neq\textsc{EMPTY}\implies\exists e.\ {e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}\wedge v={e}.\textit{in}

(B_{4.2})
Empty
deq

d_{1}=deq()\left<\textsc{EMPTY}\right>\implies\forall e.\ {e}\sqsubset{d_{1}}\implies\exists d_{2}.\ {e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\wedge{d_{2}}\sqsubset{d_{1}}

(B_{4.3})

enq

e=enq(\_)\left<v\right>\implies v=tt

(b)

History-based sequential specification. Relation
\lessdot:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
is
abstract.

Figure 18
:
State-based and history-based sequential specifications for queues. Variables
e
,
d
, and their
indexed variants, range over enqueues and dequeues, respectively.

Sequential Specification

Figure
18
shows the
sequential state-based and history-based specifications for
queues.

In the state-based specification,
axiom
A_{1}
says that a
deq
removes the head element
v
from a non-empty
queue and returns
v
. Axiom
A_{2}
says that
deq
returns

EMPTY
when the queue is empty, leaving the queue unchanged. Axiom

A_{3}
says
enq(v)
inserts
v
to the end of the queue,
returning the trivial value
tt
.

The history-based specification utilizes the
visibility relation

\lessdot
to capture a
enqueue-dequeue causal dependence between events. In particular,

{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}
means that “event
d
dequeues a value that event
e

added to the queue”. Under this interpretation,
axioms
B_{1},...,B_{4.3}
state the following expected
properties.

Axiom
B_{1}

encodes the FIFO discipline. More specifically,
if
e_{2}
enqueues a value before enqueue
e_{1}

(i.e.,
e_{2}\sqsubset e_{1}
),
but we know that
e_{1}
has already been dequeued
by
d_{1}
(i.e.,
{e_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}
),
then
e_{2}
must have been dequeued before
d_{1}
,
because
e_{2}
enqueued first, i.e.,
the first to enqueue is dequeued first.

Axiom
B_{2}
(Dequeue uniqueness) says that a enqueue is observed by at most
one dequeue.

Axiom
B_{3}
(Dependences occur in the past) says that if a
dequeue depends on an enqueue, then the enqueue executes before the dequeue.

Axioms
B_{4.1}
(Non-empty
deq
),
B_{4.2}
(Empty
deq
),
and
B_{4.3}
essentially are the counterparts of the state-based
sequential axioms
A_{1}
-
A_{3}
, respectively, as we show next.

Axiom
B_{4.1}
says that a
deq()\left<v\right>
event
d

observes an enqueue
e
that enqueued
v
.
This axiom, along with
B_{1}
-
B_{3}
, ensures that
d
relates to
e
as
in the following diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}[{v}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}({v}::{Q_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v}::{Q_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d=deq()\left<v\right>}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}

In particular: (i)
e
executes before
d
(by axiom
B_{3}
, because

{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}
), (ii) every enqueue before
e
is dequeued
before
d
(by axiom
B_{1}
), and each enqueue is dequeued exactly once (by
axiom
B_{2}
). Thus, the subqueue
Q
contains values enqueued before

e
, and since each of these values is dequeued before
d
, the subqueue

Q
is empty when
d
executes, which means that
v
is at the head
of the queue when
d
executes. Between
e
and
d
, other enqueues
could enqueue values after
v
, so that subqueue
Q_{2}
has a smaller or equal
length than
Q_{3}
. Between
e
and
d
, dequeues will remove the values before

v
, so that subqueue
Q
has a bigger or equal length than
Q_{1}
.
When
d
executes the queue is modified
from
{v}::{Q_{3}}
to
Q_{3}
.
This explains that
B_{4.1}
is essentially a
history-based version of
A_{1}
.

Similarly, axiom
B_{4.2}
states that if a

deq()\left<\textsc{EMPTY}\right>
event
d
occurs, then every enqueue
before
d
must have been dequeued before
d
, as this ensures that the
queue is empty when
d
is reached. Hence, the axiom is counterpart to

A_{2}
.

Finally, axiom
B_{4.3}
says that the output of a enqueue event is the
trivial value
tt
. The axiom imposes no conditions on the
queue, as a value can always be enqueued. In this,
B_{4.3}
is the
counterpart to
A_{3}
which also imposes no conditions on the input
queue, and posits that push’s output value is trivial. However, unlike

A_{3}
,
B_{4.3}
does not directly says that the value is enqueued
at the end of the queue, as that aspect is captured by the relationships
between enqueues and dequeues described by
B_{4.1}
.

(
Q_{103}
) Concurrent FIFO

{e_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge{e_{1}}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}{e_{2}}\implies\exists d_{2}.\ {e_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\wedge{d_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{d_{1}}

(
Q_{104}
) Dequeue uniqueness

{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge{e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\implies d_{1}=d_{2}

(
Q_{105}
) No future dependences

x\mathrel{{\prec}^{+}}y\implies{y}\not\sqsubseteq{x}

(
Q_{106}
) Return value completion

\exists v.\ \mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}\wedge(x\in T\implies v={x}.\textit{out})

(a)

Concurrent specification. Relations
\lessdot,\ltimes:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
are abstract.

Constraint relation

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\lessdot\cup\ltimes

Returns-before relation

{k_{1}}\sqsubset{k_{2}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{k_{1}}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{k_{2}}.\textit{start}

Set of terminated events

T\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{k\mid{k}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\}

Closure of terminated events

\overline{T}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{k\mid\exists t\in T.\ k\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}t\}

\mathcal{Q}_{{d_{1}},{v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{cases}\exists e.\ {e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge v={e}.\textit{in}&\text{if $v\neq\textsc{EMPTY}$}\\
\forall e.\ {d_{1}}\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}{e}\implies\exists d_{2}.\ {e}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\wedge{d_{2}}\mathrel{\ltimes}{d_{1}}&\text{if $v=\textsc{EMPTY}$}\\
\end{cases}

\mathcal{Q}_{{e},{v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}v=tt

(b)

Defined notions.

Concurrent history-based specification for queues. Variables

,

.

Concurrent Specification

.
The transformation procedure is identical to the one in

.

Axiom

for

following the replacement rules described in

:

a\sqsubset b
are replaced
with
b\not\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}a
; positive occurrences of

are replaced with
a\ltimes b
.

.

C.3
Locks

(A_{1})

(false,0)\xrightarrow{lock()\ \langle tt\rangle}(true,0)

(A_{2})

(true,c)\xrightarrow{unlock()\ \langle tt\rangle}(false,c)

(A_{3})

(false,n)\xrightarrow{reg()\ \langle tt\rangle}(false,n+1)

(A_{4})

(b,n+1)\xrightarrow{dereg()\ \langle tt\rangle}(b,n)

(a)

State-based sequential specification. The initial state is
(false,0)
.

(A_{1}^{\prime})

(false,0)\xrightarrow{lock()\ \langle tt\rangle}(true,0)

(A_{2}^{\prime})

(true,0)\xrightarrow{unlock()\ \langle tt\rangle}(false,0)

(A_{3}^{\prime})

(false,n)\xrightarrow{reg()\ \langle tt\rangle}(false,n+1)

(A_{4}^{\prime})

(false,n+1)\xrightarrow{dereg()\ \langle tt\rangle}(false,n)

(b)

Alternative equivalent state-based sequential specification. It restricts
A_{2}
with
c=0
and
A_{4}
with
b=false
. The initial state is
(false,0)
.

(B_{1})
No locks in-between

{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{l_{1}}\implies\forall l_{2}.\ {l_{2}}\sqsubseteq{u_{1}}\vee{l_{1}}\sqsubseteq{l_{2}}

(B_{2})
No unlocks in-between

{l_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{u_{1}}\implies\forall u_{2}.\ {u_{2}}\sqsubseteq{l_{1}}\vee{u_{1}}\sqsubseteq{u_{2}}

(B_{3})
No locks in-between pending reg

{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{r}\implies\forall l.\ {l}\sqsubseteq{u}\vee{r}\sqsubseteq{l}

(B_{4})
No pending regs before lock

{r}\sqsubset{l}\implies\exists d.\ {r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}\wedge{d}\sqsubset{l}

(B_{5})
Reg uniqueness

{r_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}\wedge{r_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}\implies r_{1}=r_{2}

(B_{6})
Dereg uniqueness

{r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{1}}\wedge{r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d_{2}}\implies d_{1}=d_{2}

(B_{7})
Dependences occur in the past

{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}{y}\implies{x}\sqsubset{y}

(B_{8.1})
Case
lock

l_{1}=lock()\ \langle v\rangle\implies v=tt\wedge[(\forall l_{2}.\ {l_{1}}\sqsubseteq{l_{2}})\vee(\exists u.\ {u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{l_{1}})]

(B_{8.2})
Case
unlock

u=unlock()\ \langle v\rangle\implies v=tt\wedge\exists l.\ {l}\mathrel{\lessdot}{u}

(B_{8.3})
Case
reg

r=reg()\ \langle v\rangle\implies v=tt\wedge[(\forall l.\ {r}\sqsubset{l})\vee(\exists u.\ {u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{r})]

(B_{8.4})
Case
dereg

d=dereg()\ \langle v\rangle\implies v=tt\wedge\exists r.\ {r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}

(c)

History-based sequential specification. Relation
\lessdot:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
is
abstract.

Figure 20
:
State-based and history-based sequential specifications for readers/writer locks. Variables
l
,
u
,
r
,
d
, and their
indexed variants, range over locks, unlocks, regs, and deregs, respectively. Variables
x
and
y
range over arbitrary events.

C.3.1
Readers/Writer Locks

A readers/writer lock allows concurrent access for read-only operations (readers lock),
whereas it imposes exclusive access to write operations (writer lock).
This means that multiple readers can acquire the readers lock and execute in parallel,
but only one writer can acquire the writer lock and modify the data.
When a writer acquires the writer lock, all other writers and readers are blocked until the writer releases the
writer lock.
A writer cannot acquire the writer lock if there is at least one reader that has not released the
readers lock.

The abstract state of a readers/writer lock is a tuple
(b,c)
where
b
is a boolean
that indicates if the writer lock is acquired (
true
) or not (
false
), and

c
is a natural number, counting the number of readers that have
acquired the readers lock. The readers lock is not acquired if
c=0

and acquired if
c>0
.

The data structure for a readers/writer lock consists on four methods.

lock()
acquires the writer lock if both the writer and readers lock are currently not acquired;

unlock()
releases the writer lock if it is currently locked;

reg()
(i.e., “register”) acquires the readers lock by increasing the readers count
only if the writer lock is currently not acquired;

dereg()
(i.e., “deregister”) decrements by one the readers lock only if the
readers lock is acquired. All the above methods have
the trivial output
tt
.

Sequential Specification

The methods are better described by the state-based sequential specification in
Figure
20(a)
.
In axiom
A_{1}
,
lock()
changes the writer lock to
true
(acquired)
only if the writer lock is
false
(not acquired) and the readers count is
0

(i.e., readers lock is not acquired).
In axiom
A_{2}
,
unlock()
changes the writer lock to
false
(not acquired)
only if the writer lock is
true
(acquired).
In axiom
A_{3}
,
reg()
increases the readers count by one, but only if
the writer lock is
false
(not acquired). In axiom
A_{4}
,
dereg()
decreases
the readers count by one, but only if the readers count is bigger than
0
(i.e.,
readers lock is acquired).

Figure
20(b)

shows an alternative state-based specification which restricts
the readers counter
c
in
A_{2}
to
0
and the boolean

b
in
A_{4}
to
false
. One can easily show that both
state-based specifications in
Figures
20(a)
and

20(b)

are equivalent: (1) we must have
c=0
in

A_{2}
because once a lock executes, no register or deregister can execute
because the lock set the boolean to
true
and the counter to
0
; therefore,
when the unlock executes, the counter will be
0
; (2) we must have
b=false

in
A_{4}
because once a register executes, no lock or unlock can execute
because the register increased the counter and left the boolean in the
false
state;
therefore, when the deregister executes, the boolean will still be
false
.

The axioms in
Figure
20(a)

impose less constraints on the states, because
A_{2}
does not require any
constraint on the readers counter, and
A_{4}
does not require any constraint
on the boolean. This will make easier to explain the connection between
the visibility relation and the state constraints in
A_{1}
-
A_{4}
once we move
to the history-based specification.
Nevertheless, when illustrating executions in a history, we will
use the axioms in
Figure
20(b)
.

Figure
20(c)

shows the corresponding history-based sequential specification.
Again, the specification utilizes the
visibility relation

\lessdot
to capture a
causal dependence between events. In particular,

{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}{y}
means that “event
y
can execute because of event
x
”.
The axioms
B_{1}
-
B_{8.4}
make use of four cases for the relation

\lessdot
, each case corresponding to a state constraint
in the axioms
A_{1}
-
A_{4}
, as we explain next.

Case
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{l}
for unlock
u
and lock
l
. The statement

{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{l}
means
that
l
can execute because
u
released the writer lock, i.e.,
the writer lock is not acquired, which is a constraint in axiom
A_{1}
.
Note that axiom
A_{1}
also requires that the readers lock be
not acquired, but we will see later that axiom
B_{4}
models this
constraint, so that there is no need to introduce a case in
the visibility relation that relates locks with registers
and deregisters.

Case
{l}\mathrel{\lessdot}{u}
for lock
l
and unlock
u
.
The statement
{l}\mathrel{\lessdot}{u}
means
that
u
can execute because
l
acquired the writer lock, i.e.,
the writer lock is acquired, which is the only constraint in axiom
A_{2}
.

Case
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{r}
for unlock
u
and register
r
.
The statement
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{r}
means
that
r
can execute because
u
released the writer lock, i.e.,
the writer lock is not acquired, which is the only constraint in axiom
A_{3}
.

Case
{r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}
for register
r
and deregister
d
.
The statement
{r}\mathrel{\lessdot}{d}
means
that
d
can execute because
r
increased the readers count, i.e.,
the readers lock is acquired, which is the only constraint in axiom
A_{4}
.

Under this interpretation of the
\lessdot
relation,
axioms
B_{1},...,B_{8.4}
state the following properties.

B_{1}
states that if lock
l_{1}
can execute because unlock
u_{1}

released the writer lock (
{u_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{l_{1}}
), then any other lock

l_{2}
cannot execute between
u_{1}
and
l_{1}
, otherwise either: (1) the
writer lock is already acquired by
l_{2}
when
l_{1}
tries to execute, or (2)

l_{2}
was actually released by some other unlock
u_{2}
, but then this would contradict
that
u_{1}
is the
cause
for
l_{1}
.

B_{2}
states something identical to
B_{1}
but for unlocks:
if unlock
u_{1}
can execute because lock
l_{1}

acquired the writer lock (
{l_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}{u_{1}}
), then any other unlock

u_{2}
cannot execute between
l_{1}
and
u_{1}
.

B_{3}
states that if a register
r
can execute because unlock

u
released the writer lock (
{u}\mathrel{\lessdot}{r}
) then no lock
l

can start between
u
and
r
, otherwise either: (1) the
writer lock is already acquired by
l
when
r
tries to execute, or (2)

l
was actually released by some other unlock
u^{\prime}
, but then this would contradict
that
u
is the
cause
for
r
.

B_{4}
states that any register
r
before a lock
l
must have been deregistered by
some
d
before the lock executes, otherwise, the readers counter would not be zero
when lock
l
tries to execute. Note that
B_{4}
encodes the state constraint
in axiom
A_{1}
that states
lock()
cannot acquire the writer lock if the readers lock
is acquired.

B_{5}
and
B_{6}
state that the visibility relation pairs each deregister
with a unique register, and each register with a unique deregister. In other words,
each decrease in the readers counter comes from a unique increase, and
each increase in the readers counter can only be paired with a unique decrease.

B_{7}
is the standard axiom stating that any dependence of an event
y
must execute before
y
.

Axioms
B_{8.1}
-
B_{8.4}
are the history-based version of axioms
A_{1}
-
A_{4}
.
As we explain next.

Axiom
B_{8.1}
states that either lock
l_{1}=lock()
is the first lock in the history
or there is an unlock
u
observed by
l_{1}
(which happens when
l_{1}
is not the first lock in the history).

In case
l_{1}
is the first lock in the history, the execution looks as follows,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}=lock()\langle tt\rangle}

We must have that the boolean does not change from
false
to
true
before
l_{1}
, because there is no lock before
l_{1}
.
Also, registers and deregisters do not change the boolean. The readers counter could increase and decrease
arbitrarily before
l_{1}
, but (1)
B_{4}
ensures that every register is deregistered before
l_{1}
, and (2)
each increase of the readers counter is decreased exactly once by
B_{5}
and
B_{6}
. Therefore, just before

l_{1}
executes, the readers counter must be zero and the boolean is
false
, and
l_{1}
executes as in
A_{1}
.

In case
l_{1}
observes an unlock
u
, the execution looks as follows,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}=lock()\langle tt\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}

First,
u
executes before
l_{1}
by
B_{7}
.
u
changes the boolean to
false
.
We must have that the boolean does not change from
false
to
true
in between
u
and
l_{1}
,
because
B_{1}
implies that there is no lock in between
u
and
l_{1}
.
Also, registers and deregisters do not change the boolean.
The readers counter could increase and decrease
arbitrarily in between
u
and
l_{1}
, but (1)
B_{4}
ensures that every register is deregistered before
l_{1}
, and (2)
each increase of the readers counter is decreased exactly once by
B_{5}
and
B_{6}
. Therefore, just before

l_{1}
executes, the readers counter must be zero and the boolean is
false
, and
l_{1}
executes as in
A_{1}
.

Axiom
B_{8.2}
states that any unlock
u=unlock()
must observe a lock
l
. The execution will look as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u=unlock()\langle tt\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}

(20)

First,
l
executes before
u
by
B_{7}
.
l
changes the state to
(true,0)
. We now show that there cannot exist
an event between
l
and
u
. Axiom
B_{2}
states that there cannot exist an unlock between
l
and
u
. It remains to show
that there cannot exist a lock, register, and deregister between
l
and
u
.

Suppose there is a lock
l_{2}
in between
l
and
u
. Since
l_{2}
is not the first lock (i.e.,
l
executes before
l_{2}
),
axiom
B_{8.1}
states that
l_{2}
must be observing an unlock
u_{2}
that executes before
l_{2}
by
B_{7}
.
If
u_{2}
executes before
l
, i.e.,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u=unlock()\langle tt\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}

then
l
is in between
u_{2}
and
l_{2}
, which contradicts
B_{1}
.

If
u_{2}
executes after
l
, i.e.,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(true,0)\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(false,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u=unlock()\langle tt\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lessdot}

then

is in between

and

, which contradicts

.

Suppose there is a register
r
in between

and

. Since

executes before

,

states that

must be observing an unlock

that executes before

by

.
If
